Title: 254A, Notes 4: Some sieve theory

URL Source: https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/

Published Time: 2015-01-22T00:50:20+00:00

Markdown Content:
Many problems in non-multiplicative prime number theory can be recast as _sieving_ problems. Consider for instance the problem of counting the number ![Image 1: {N(x)}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BN%28x%29%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) of pairs of twin primes ![Image 2: {p,p+2}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bp%2Cp%2B2%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) contained in ![Image 3: {[x/2,x]}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Bx%2F2%2Cx%5D%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) for some large ![Image 4: {x}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bx%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002); note that the claim that ![Image 5: {N(x) > 0}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BN%28x%29+%3E+0%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) for arbitrarily large ![Image 6: {x}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bx%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) is equivalent to the twin prime conjecture. One can obtain this count by any of the following variants of the [sieve of Eratosthenes](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sieve_of_Eratosthenes):

1.    Let ![Image 7: {A}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BA%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) be the set of natural numbers in ![Image 8: {[x/2,x-2]}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Bx%2F2%2Cx-2%5D%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002). For each prime ![Image 9: {p \leq \sqrt{x}}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bp+%5Cleq+%5Csqrt%7Bx%7D%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002), let ![Image 10: {E_p}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BE_p%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) be the union of the residue classes ![Image 11: {0\ (p)}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B0%5C+%28p%29%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) and ![Image 12: {-2\ (p)}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B-2%5C+%28p%29%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002). Then ![Image 13: {N(x)}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BN%28x%29%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) is the cardinality of the _sifted set_![Image 14: {A \backslash \bigcup_{p \leq \sqrt{x}} E_p}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BA+%5Cbackslash+%5Cbigcup_%7Bp+%5Cleq+%5Csqrt%7Bx%7D%7D+E_p%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002). 
2.    Let ![Image 15: {A}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BA%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) be the set of primes in ![Image 16: {[x/2,x-2]}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Bx%2F2%2Cx-2%5D%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002). For each prime ![Image 17: {p \leq \sqrt{x}}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bp+%5Cleq+%5Csqrt%7Bx%7D%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002), let ![Image 18: {E_p}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BE_p%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) be the residue class ![Image 19: {-2\ (p)}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B-2%5C+%28p%29%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002). Then ![Image 20: {N(x)}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BN%28x%29%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) is the cardinality of the _sifted set_![Image 21: {A \backslash \bigcup_{p \leq \sqrt{x}} E_p}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BA+%5Cbackslash+%5Cbigcup_%7Bp+%5Cleq+%5Csqrt%7Bx%7D%7D+E_p%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002). 
3.    Let ![Image 22: {A}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BA%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) be the set of primes in ![Image 23: {[x/2+2,x]}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Bx%2F2%2B2%2Cx%5D%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002). For each prime ![Image 24: {p \leq \sqrt{x}}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bp+%5Cleq+%5Csqrt%7Bx%7D%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002), let ![Image 25: {E_p}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BE_p%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) be the residue class ![Image 26: {2\ (p)}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B2%5C+%28p%29%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002). Then ![Image 27: {N(x)}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BN%28x%29%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) is the cardinality of the _sifted set_![Image 28: {A \backslash \bigcup_{p \leq \sqrt{x}} E_p}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BA+%5Cbackslash+%5Cbigcup_%7Bp+%5Cleq+%5Csqrt%7Bx%7D%7D+E_p%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002). 
4.    Let ![Image 29: {A}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BA%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) be the set ![Image 30: {\{ n(n+2): x/2 \leq n \leq x-2 \}}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5C%7B+n%28n%2B2%29%3A+x%2F2+%5Cleq+n+%5Cleq+x-2+%5C%7D%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002). For each prime ![Image 31: {p \leq \sqrt{x}}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bp+%5Cleq+%5Csqrt%7Bx%7D%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002), let ![Image 32: {E_p}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BE_p%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) be the residue class ![Image 33: {0\ (p)}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B0%5C+%28p%29%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) Then ![Image 34: {N(x)}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BN%28x%29%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) is the cardinality of the _sifted set_![Image 35: {A \backslash \bigcup_{p \leq \sqrt{x}} E_p}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BA+%5Cbackslash+%5Cbigcup_%7Bp+%5Cleq+%5Csqrt%7Bx%7D%7D+E_p%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002). 

> **Exercise 1** Develop similar sifting formulations of the other three [Landau problems](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Landau%27s_problems).

In view of these sieving interpretations of number-theoretic problems, it becomes natural to try to estimate the size of sifted sets ![Image 36: {A \backslash \bigcup_{p | P} E_p}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BA+%5Cbackslash+%5Cbigcup_%7Bp+%7C+P%7D+E_p%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) for various finite sets ![Image 37: {A}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BA%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) of integers, and subsets ![Image 38: {E_p}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BE_p%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) of integers indexed by primes ![Image 39: {p}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bp%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) dividing some squarefree natural number ![Image 40: {P}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BP%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) (which, in the above examples, would be the product of all primes up to ![Image 41: {\sqrt{x}}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Csqrt%7Bx%7D%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)). As we see in the above examples, the sets ![Image 42: {E_p}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BE_p%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) in applications are typically the union of one or more residue classes modulo ![Image 43: {p}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bp%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002), but we will work at a more abstract level of generality here by treating ![Image 44: {E_p}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BE_p%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) as more or less arbitrary sets of integers, without caring too much about the arithmetic structure of such sets.

It turns out to be conceptually more natural to [replace sets by functions](http://www.tricki.org/article/Turn_sets_into_functions), and to consider the more general the task of estimating sifted _sums_[](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/)

[![Image 45: \displaystyle \sum_{n \in {\bf Z}} a_n 1_{n \not \in \bigcup_{p | P} E_p} \ \ \ \ \ (1)](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle++%5Csum_%7Bn+%5Cin+%7B%5Cbf+Z%7D%7D+a_n+1_%7Bn+%5Cnot+%5Cin+%5Cbigcup_%7Bp+%7C+P%7D+E_p%7D+%5C+%5C+%5C+%5C+%5C+%281%29&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/)
[](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/) for some finitely supported sequence ![Image 46: {(a_n)_{n \in {\bf Z}}}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%28a_n%29_%7Bn+%5Cin+%7B%5Cbf+Z%7D%7D%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) of non-negative numbers; the previous combinatorial sifting problem then corresponds to the indicator function case ![Image 47: {a_n=1_{n \in A}}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Ba_n%3D1_%7Bn+%5Cin+A%7D%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002). (One could also use other index sets here than the integers ![Image 48: {{\bf Z}}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%7B%5Cbf+Z%7D%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) if desired; for much of sieve theory the index set and its subsets ![Image 49: {E_p}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BE_p%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) are treated as abstract sets, so the exact arithmetic structure of these sets is not of primary importance.)

Continuing with twin primes as a running example, we thus have the following sample sieving problem:

> **Problem 2 (Sieving problem for twin primes)**[](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/) Let ![Image 50: {x, z \geq 1}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bx%2C+z+%5Cgeq+1%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002), and let ![Image 51: {\pi_2(x,z)}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Cpi_2%28x%2Cz%29%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) denote the number of natural numbers ![Image 52: {n \leq x}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bn+%5Cleq+x%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) which avoid the residue classes ![Image 53: {0, -2\ (p)}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B0%2C+-2%5C+%28p%29%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) for all primes ![Image 54: {p < z}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bp+%3C+z%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002). In other words, we have
> 
> 
> ![Image 55: \displaystyle  \pi_2(x,z) := \sum_{n \in {\bf Z}} a_n 1_{n \not \in \bigcup_{p | P(z)} E_p} ](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle++%5Cpi_2%28x%2Cz%29+%3A%3D+%5Csum_%7Bn+%5Cin+%7B%5Cbf+Z%7D%7D+a_n+1_%7Bn+%5Cnot+%5Cin+%5Cbigcup_%7Bp+%7C+P%28z%29%7D+E_p%7D+&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)
> 
> 
> where ![Image 56: {a_n := 1_{n \in [1,x]}}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Ba_n+%3A%3D+1_%7Bn+%5Cin+%5B1%2Cx%5D%7D%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002), ![Image 57: {P(z) := \prod_{p < z} p}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BP%28z%29+%3A%3D+%5Cprod_%7Bp+%3C+z%7D+p%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) is the product of all the primes strictly less than ![Image 58: {z}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bz%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) (we omit ![Image 59: {z}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bz%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) itself for minor technical reasons), and ![Image 60: {E_p}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BE_p%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) is the union of the residue classes ![Image 61: {0, -2\ (p)}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B0%2C+-2%5C+%28p%29%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002). Obtain upper and lower bounds on ![Image 62: {\pi_2(x,z)}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Cpi_2%28x%2Cz%29%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) which are as strong as possible in the asymptotic regime where ![Image 63: {x}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bx%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) goes to infinity and the _sifting level_![Image 64: {z}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bz%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) grows with ![Image 65: {x}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bx%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) (ideally we would like ![Image 66: {z}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bz%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) to grow as fast as ![Image 67: {\sqrt{x}}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Csqrt%7Bx%7D%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)).

From the preceding discussion we know that the number of twin prime pairs ![Image 68: {p,p+2}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bp%2Cp%2B2%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) in ![Image 69: {(x/2,x]}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%28x%2F2%2Cx%5D%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) is equal to ![Image 70: {\pi_2(x-2,\sqrt{x}) - \pi_2(x/2,\sqrt{x})}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Cpi_2%28x-2%2C%5Csqrt%7Bx%7D%29+-+%5Cpi_2%28x%2F2%2C%5Csqrt%7Bx%7D%29%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002), if ![Image 71: {x}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bx%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) is not a perfect square; one also easily sees that the number of twin prime pairs in ![Image 72: {[1,x]}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5B1%2Cx%5D%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) is at least ![Image 73: {\pi_2(x-2,\sqrt{x})}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Cpi_2%28x-2%2C%5Csqrt%7Bx%7D%29%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002), again if ![Image 74: {x}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bx%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) is not a perfect square. Thus we see that a sufficiently good answer to Problem [2](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/#twin) would resolve the twin prime conjecture, particularly if we can get the sifting level ![Image 75: {z}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bz%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) to be as large as ![Image 76: {\sqrt{x}}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Csqrt%7Bx%7D%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002).

We return now to the general problem of estimating [(1)](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/#fep). We may expand [](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/)

[![Image 77: \displaystyle 1_{n \not \in \bigcup_{p | P} E_p} = \prod_{p | P} (1 - 1_{E_p}(n)) \ \ \ \ \ (2)](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle++1_%7Bn+%5Cnot+%5Cin+%5Cbigcup_%7Bp+%7C+P%7D+E_p%7D+%3D+%5Cprod_%7Bp+%7C+P%7D+%281+-+1_%7BE_p%7D%28n%29%29+%5C+%5C+%5C+%5C+%5C+%282%29&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/)
![Image 78: \displaystyle  = \sum_{k=0}^\infty (-1)^k \sum_{p_1 \dots p_k|P: p_1 < \dots < p_k} 1_{E_{p_1}} \dots 1_{E_{p_k}}(n)](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle++%3D+%5Csum_%7Bk%3D0%7D%5E%5Cinfty+%28-1%29%5Ek+%5Csum_%7Bp_1+%5Cdots+p_k%7CP%3A+p_1+%3C+%5Cdots+%3C+p_k%7D+1_%7BE_%7Bp_1%7D%7D+%5Cdots+1_%7BE_%7Bp_k%7D%7D%28n%29&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)

![Image 79: \displaystyle  = \sum_{d|P} \mu(d) 1_{E_d}(n)](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle++%3D+%5Csum_%7Bd%7CP%7D+%5Cmu%28d%29+1_%7BE_d%7D%28n%29&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)

where ![Image 80: {E_d := \bigcap_{p|d} E_p}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BE_d+%3A%3D+%5Cbigcap_%7Bp%7Cd%7D+E_p%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) (with the convention that ![Image 81: {E_1={\bf Z}}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BE_1%3D%7B%5Cbf+Z%7D%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)). We thus arrive at the [Legendre sieve identity](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Legendre_sieve)[](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/)

[![Image 82: \displaystyle \sum_{n \in {\bf Z}} a_n 1_{n \not \in \bigcup_{p | P} E_p} = \sum_{d|P} \mu(d) \sum_{n \in E_d} a_n. \ \ \ \ \ (3)](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle++%5Csum_%7Bn+%5Cin+%7B%5Cbf+Z%7D%7D+a_n+1_%7Bn+%5Cnot+%5Cin+%5Cbigcup_%7Bp+%7C+P%7D+E_p%7D+%3D+%5Csum_%7Bd%7CP%7D+%5Cmu%28d%29+%5Csum_%7Bn+%5Cin+E_d%7D+a_n.+%5C+%5C+%5C+%5C+%5C+%283%29&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/)
[](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/) Specialising to the case of an indicator function ![Image 83: {a_n=1_{n \in A}}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Ba_n%3D1_%7Bn+%5Cin+A%7D%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002), we recover the [inclusion-exclusion formula](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inclusion%E2%80%93exclusion_principle)

![Image 84: \displaystyle  |A \backslash \bigcup_{p|P} E_p| = \sum_{d|P} \mu(d) |A \cap E_d|.](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle++%7CA+%5Cbackslash+%5Cbigcup_%7Bp%7CP%7D+E_p%7C+%3D+%5Csum_%7Bd%7CP%7D+%5Cmu%28d%29+%7CA+%5Ccap+E_d%7C.&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)

Such exact sieving formulae are already satisfactory for controlling sifted sets or sifted sums when the amount of sieving is relatively small compared to the size of ![Image 85: {A}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BA%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002). For instance, let us return to the running example in Problem [2](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/#twin) for some ![Image 86: {x,z \geq 1}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bx%2Cz+%5Cgeq+1%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002). Observe that each ![Image 87: {E_p}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BE_p%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) in this example consists of ![Image 88: {\omega(p)}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Comega%28p%29%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) residue classes modulo ![Image 89: {p}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bp%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002), where ![Image 90: {\omega(p)}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Comega%28p%29%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) is defined to equal ![Image 91: {1}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B1%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) when ![Image 92: {p=2}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bp%3D2%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) and ![Image 93: {2}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B2%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) when ![Image 94: {p}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bp%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) is odd. By the Chinese remainder theorem, this implies that for each ![Image 95: {d|P(z)}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bd%7CP%28z%29%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002), ![Image 96: {E_d}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BE_d%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) consists of ![Image 97: {\prod_{p|d} \omega(p)}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Cprod_%7Bp%7Cd%7D+%5Comega%28p%29%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) residue classes modulo ![Image 98: {d}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bd%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002). Using the basic bound [](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/)

[![Image 99: \displaystyle \sum_{n \leq x: n = a\ (q)} 1 = \frac{x}{q} + O(1) \ \ \ \ \ (4)](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle++%5Csum_%7Bn+%5Cleq+x%3A+n+%3D+a%5C+%28q%29%7D+1+%3D+%5Cfrac%7Bx%7D%7Bq%7D+%2B+O%281%29+%5C+%5C+%5C+%5C+%5C+%284%29&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/)
[](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/) for any ![Image 100: {x > 0}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bx+%3E+0%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) and any residue class ![Image 101: {a\ (q)}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Ba%5C+%28q%29%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002), we conclude that [](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/)

[![Image 102: \displaystyle \sum_{n \in E_d} a_n = g(d) x + O( \prod_{p|d} \omega(p) ) \ \ \ \ \ (5)](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle++%5Csum_%7Bn+%5Cin+E_d%7D+a_n+%3D+g%28d%29+x+%2B+O%28+%5Cprod_%7Bp%7Cd%7D+%5Comega%28p%29+%29+%5C+%5C+%5C+%5C+%5C+%285%29&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/)
[](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/) for any ![Image 103: {d|P(z)}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bd%7CP%28z%29%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002), where ![Image 104: {g}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bg%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) is the multiplicative function

![Image 105: \displaystyle  g(d) := \prod_{p|d: p|P(z)} \frac{\omega(p)}{p}.](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle++g%28d%29+%3A%3D+%5Cprod_%7Bp%7Cd%3A+p%7CP%28z%29%7D+%5Cfrac%7B%5Comega%28p%29%7D%7Bp%7D.&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)

Since ![Image 106: {\omega(p) \leq 2}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Comega%28p%29+%5Cleq+2%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) and there are at most ![Image 107: {\pi(z)}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Cpi%28z%29%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) primes dividing ![Image 108: {P(z)}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BP%28z%29%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002), we may crudely bound ![Image 109: {\prod_{p|d} \omega(p) \leq 2^{\pi(z)}}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Cprod_%7Bp%7Cd%7D+%5Comega%28p%29+%5Cleq+2%5E%7B%5Cpi%28z%29%7D%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002), thus [](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/)

[![Image 110: \displaystyle \sum_{n \in E_d} a_n = g(d) x + O( 2^{\pi(z)} ). \ \ \ \ \ (6)](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle++%5Csum_%7Bn+%5Cin+E_d%7D+a_n+%3D+g%28d%29+x+%2B+O%28+2%5E%7B%5Cpi%28z%29%7D+%29.+%5C+%5C+%5C+%5C+%5C+%286%29&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/)
[](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/) Also, the number of divisors of ![Image 111: {P(z)}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BP%28z%29%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) is at most ![Image 112: {2^{\pi(z)}}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B2%5E%7B%5Cpi%28z%29%7D%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002). From the Legendre sieve [(3)](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/#legendre), we thus conclude that

![Image 113: \displaystyle  \pi_2(x,z) = (\sum_{d|P(z)} \mu(d) g(d) x) + O( 4^{\pi(z)} ).](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle++%5Cpi_2%28x%2Cz%29+%3D+%28%5Csum_%7Bd%7CP%28z%29%7D+%5Cmu%28d%29+g%28d%29+x%29+%2B+O%28+4%5E%7B%5Cpi%28z%29%7D+%29.&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)

We can factorise the main term to obtain

![Image 114: \displaystyle  \pi_2(x,z) = x \prod_{p < z} (1-\frac{\omega(p)}{p}) + O( 4^{\pi(z)} ).](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle++%5Cpi_2%28x%2Cz%29+%3D+x+%5Cprod_%7Bp+%3C+z%7D+%281-%5Cfrac%7B%5Comega%28p%29%7D%7Bp%7D%29+%2B+O%28+4%5E%7B%5Cpi%28z%29%7D+%29.&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)

This is compatible with the heuristic [](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/)

[![Image 115: \displaystyle \pi_2(x,z) \approx x \prod_{p < z} (1-\frac{\omega(p)}{p}) \ \ \ \ \ (7)](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle++%5Cpi_2%28x%2Cz%29+%5Capprox+x+%5Cprod_%7Bp+%3C+z%7D+%281-%5Cfrac%7B%5Comega%28p%29%7D%7Bp%7D%29+%5C+%5C+%5C+%5C+%5C+%287%29&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/)
[](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/) coming from the equidistribution of residues principle (Section 3 of [Supplement 4](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/04/254a-supplement-4-probabilistic-models-and-heuristics-for-the-primes-optional/)), bearing in mind (from the modified Cramér model, see Section 1 of [Supplement 4](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/04/254a-supplement-4-probabilistic-models-and-heuristics-for-the-primes-optional/)) that we expect this heuristic to become inaccurate when ![Image 116: {z}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bz%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) becomes very large. We can simplify the right-hand side of [(7)](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/#heuristic) by recalling the [twin prime constant](http://mathworld.wolfram.com/TwinPrimesConstant.html)

![Image 117: \displaystyle  \Pi_2 := \prod_{p>2} (1 - \frac{1}{(p-1)^2}) = 0.6601618\dots](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle++%5CPi_2+%3A%3D+%5Cprod_%7Bp%3E2%7D+%281+-+%5Cfrac%7B1%7D%7B%28p-1%29%5E2%7D%29+%3D+0.6601618%5Cdots&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)

(see equation (7) from [Supplement 4](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/04/254a-supplement-4-probabilistic-models-and-heuristics-for-the-primes-optional/)); note that

![Image 118: \displaystyle  \prod_p (1-\frac{1}{p})^{-2} (1-\frac{\omega(p)}{p}) = 2 \Pi_2](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle++%5Cprod_p+%281-%5Cfrac%7B1%7D%7Bp%7D%29%5E%7B-2%7D+%281-%5Cfrac%7B%5Comega%28p%29%7D%7Bp%7D%29+%3D+2+%5CPi_2&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)

so from Mertens’ third theorem (Theorem 42 from [Notes 1](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2014/11/23/254a-notes-1-elementary-multiplicative-number-theory/)) one has [](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/)

[![Image 119: \displaystyle \prod_{p < z} (1-\frac{\omega(p)}{p}) = (2\Pi_2+o(1)) \frac{1}{(e^\gamma \log z)^2} \ \ \ \ \ (8)](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle++%5Cprod_%7Bp+%3C+z%7D+%281-%5Cfrac%7B%5Comega%28p%29%7D%7Bp%7D%29+%3D+%282%5CPi_2%2Bo%281%29%29+%5Cfrac%7B1%7D%7B%28e%5E%5Cgamma+%5Clog+z%29%5E2%7D+%5C+%5C+%5C+%5C+%5C+%288%29&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/)
[](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/) as ![Image 120: {z \rightarrow \infty}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bz+%5Crightarrow+%5Cinfty%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002). Bounding ![Image 121: {4^{\pi(z)}}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B4%5E%7B%5Cpi%28z%29%7D%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) crudely by ![Image 122: {\exp(o(z))}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Cexp%28o%28z%29%29%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002), we conclude in particular that

![Image 123: \displaystyle  \pi_2(x,z) = (2\Pi_2 +o(1)) \frac{x}{(e^\gamma \log z)^2}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle++%5Cpi_2%28x%2Cz%29+%3D+%282%5CPi_2+%2Bo%281%29%29+%5Cfrac%7Bx%7D%7B%28e%5E%5Cgamma+%5Clog+z%29%5E2%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)

when ![Image 124: {x,z \rightarrow \infty}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bx%2Cz+%5Crightarrow+%5Cinfty%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) with ![Image 125: {z = O(\log x)}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bz+%3D+O%28%5Clog+x%29%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002). This is somewhat encouraging for the purposes of getting a sufficiently good answer to Problem [2](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/#twin) to resolve the twin prime conjecture, but note that ![Image 126: {z}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bz%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) is currently far too small: one needs to get ![Image 127: {z}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bz%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) as large as ![Image 128: {\sqrt{x}}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Csqrt%7Bx%7D%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) before one is counting twin primes, and currently ![Image 129: {z}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bz%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) can only get as large as ![Image 130: {\log x}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Clog+x%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002).

The problem is that the number of terms in the Legendre sieve [(3)](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/#legendre) basically grows exponentially in ![Image 131: {z}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bz%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002), and so the error terms in [(4)](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/#1-equi) accumulate to an unacceptable extent once ![Image 132: {z}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bz%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) is significantly larger than ![Image 133: {\log x}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Clog+x%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002). An alternative way to phrase this problem is that the estimate [(4)](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/#1-equi) is only expected to be truly useful in the regime ![Image 134: {q=o(x)}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bq%3Do%28x%29%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002); on the other hand, the moduli ![Image 135: {d}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bd%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) appearing in [(3)](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/#legendre) can be as large as ![Image 136: {P}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BP%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002), which grows exponentially in ![Image 137: {z}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bz%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) by the prime number theorem.

To resolve this problem, it is thus natural to try to _truncate_ the Legendre sieve, in such a way that one only uses information about the sums ![Image 138: {\sum_{n \in E_d} a_n}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Csum_%7Bn+%5Cin+E_d%7D+a_n%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) for a relatively small number of divisors ![Image 139: {d}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bd%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) of ![Image 140: {P}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BP%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002), such as those ![Image 141: {d}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bd%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) which are below a certain threshold ![Image 142: {D}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BD%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002). This leads to the following general sieving problem:

> **Problem 3 (General sieving problem)**[](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/) Let ![Image 143: {P}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BP%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) be a squarefree natural number, and let ![Image 144: {{\mathcal D}}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%7B%5Cmathcal+D%7D%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) be a set of divisors of ![Image 145: {P}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BP%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002). For each prime ![Image 146: {p}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bp%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) dividing ![Image 147: {P}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BP%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002), let ![Image 148: {E_p}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BE_p%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) be a set of integers, and define ![Image 149: {E_d := \bigcap_{p|d} E_p}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BE_d+%3A%3D+%5Cbigcap_%7Bp%7Cd%7D+E_p%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) for all ![Image 150: {d|P}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bd%7CP%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) (with the convention that ![Image 151: {E_1={\bf Z}}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BE_1%3D%7B%5Cbf+Z%7D%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)). Suppose that ![Image 152: {(a_n)_{n \in {\bf Z}}}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%28a_n%29_%7Bn+%5Cin+%7B%5Cbf+Z%7D%7D%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) is an (unknown) finitely supported sequence of non-negative reals, whose sums [](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/)
> 
> [![Image 153: \displaystyle X_d := \sum_{n \in E_d} a_n \ \ \ \ \ (9)](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle++X_d+%3A%3D+%5Csum_%7Bn+%5Cin+E_d%7D+a_n+%5C+%5C+%5C+%5C+%5C+%289%29&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/)
> [](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/) are known for all ![Image 154: {d \in {\mathcal D}}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bd+%5Cin+%7B%5Cmathcal+D%7D%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002). What are the best upper and lower bounds one can conclude on the quantity [(1)](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/#fep)?

Here is a simple example of this type of problem (corresponding to the case ![Image 155: {P = 6}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BP+%3D+6%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002), ![Image 156: {{\mathcal D} = \{1, 2, 3\}}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%7B%5Cmathcal+D%7D+%3D+%5C%7B1%2C+2%2C+3%5C%7D%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002), ![Image 157: {X_1 = 100}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BX_1+%3D+100%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002), ![Image 158: {X_2 = 60}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BX_2+%3D+60%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002), and ![Image 159: {X_3 = 10}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BX_3+%3D+10%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)):

> **Exercise 4**[](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/) Let ![Image 160: {(a_n)_{n \in {\bf Z}}}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%28a_n%29_%7Bn+%5Cin+%7B%5Cbf+Z%7D%7D%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) be a finitely supported sequence of non-negative reals such that ![Image 161: {\sum_{n \in {\bf Z}} a_n = 100}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Csum_%7Bn+%5Cin+%7B%5Cbf+Z%7D%7D+a_n+%3D+100%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002), ![Image 162: {\sum_{n \in {\bf Z}: 2|n} a_n = 60}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Csum_%7Bn+%5Cin+%7B%5Cbf+Z%7D%3A+2%7Cn%7D+a_n+%3D+60%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002), and ![Image 163: {\sum_{n \in {\bf Z}: 3|n} a_n = 10}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Csum_%7Bn+%5Cin+%7B%5Cbf+Z%7D%3A+3%7Cn%7D+a_n+%3D+10%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002). Show that
> 
> 
> ![Image 164: \displaystyle  30 \leq \sum_{n \in {\bf Z}: (n,6)=1} a_n \leq 40](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle++30+%5Cleq+%5Csum_%7Bn+%5Cin+%7B%5Cbf+Z%7D%3A+%28n%2C6%29%3D1%7D+a_n+%5Cleq+40&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)
> 
> 
> and give counterexamples to show that these bounds cannot be improved in general, even when ![Image 165: {a_n}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Ba_n%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) is an indicator function sequence.

Problem [3](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/#sivp) is an example of a [linear programming](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Linear_programming) problem. By using [linear programming duality](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Linear_programming#Duality) (as encapsulated by results such as the [Hahn-Banach theorem](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hahn%E2%80%93Banach_theorem), the [separating hyperplane theorem](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Separating_hyperplane_theorem), or the [Farkas lemma](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Farkas%27_lemma)), we can rephrase the above problem in terms of _upper and lower bound sieves_:

> **Theorem 5 (Dual sieve problem)**[](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/) Let ![Image 166: {P, {\mathcal D}, E_p, E_d, X_d}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BP%2C+%7B%5Cmathcal+D%7D%2C+E_p%2C+E_d%2C+X_d%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) be as in Problem [3](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/#sivp). We assume that Problem [3](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/#sivp) is _feasible_, in the sense that there exists at least one finitely supported sequence ![Image 167: {(a_n)_{n \in {\bf Z}}}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%28a_n%29_%7Bn+%5Cin+%7B%5Cbf+Z%7D%7D%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) of non-negative reals obeying the constraints in that problem. Define an (normalised) _upper bound sieve_ to be a function ![Image 168: {\nu^+: {\bf Z} \rightarrow {\bf R}}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Cnu%5E%2B%3A+%7B%5Cbf+Z%7D+%5Crightarrow+%7B%5Cbf+R%7D%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) of the form
> 
> 
> ![Image 169: \displaystyle  \nu^+ = \sum_{d \in {\mathcal D}} \lambda^+_d 1_{E_d}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle++%5Cnu%5E%2B+%3D+%5Csum_%7Bd+%5Cin+%7B%5Cmathcal+D%7D%7D+%5Clambda%5E%2B_d+1_%7BE_d%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)
> 
> 
> for some coefficients ![Image 170: {\lambda^+_d \in {\bf R}}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Clambda%5E%2B_d+%5Cin+%7B%5Cbf+R%7D%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002), and obeying the pointwise lower bound [](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/)
> 
> [![Image 171: \displaystyle \nu^+(n) \geq 1_{n \not \in\bigcup_{p|P} E_p}(n) \ \ \ \ \ (10)](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle++%5Cnu%5E%2B%28n%29+%5Cgeq+1_%7Bn+%5Cnot+%5Cin%5Cbigcup_%7Bp%7CP%7D+E_p%7D%28n%29+%5C+%5C+%5C+%5C+%5C+%2810%29&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/)
> [](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/) for all ![Image 172: {n \in {\bf Z}}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bn+%5Cin+%7B%5Cbf+Z%7D%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) (in particular ![Image 173: {\nu^+}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Cnu%5E%2B%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) is non-negative). Similarly, define a (normalised) _lower bound sieve_ to be a function ![Image 174: {\nu^-: {\bf Z} \rightarrow {\bf R}}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Cnu%5E-%3A+%7B%5Cbf+Z%7D+%5Crightarrow+%7B%5Cbf+R%7D%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) of the form
> 
> 
> ![Image 175: \displaystyle  \nu^-(n) = \sum_{d \in {\mathcal D}} \lambda^-_d 1_{E_d}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle++%5Cnu%5E-%28n%29+%3D+%5Csum_%7Bd+%5Cin+%7B%5Cmathcal+D%7D%7D+%5Clambda%5E-_d+1_%7BE_d%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)
> 
> 
> for some coefficients ![Image 176: {\lambda^-_d \in {\bf R}}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Clambda%5E-_d+%5Cin+%7B%5Cbf+R%7D%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002), and obeying the pointwise upper bound
> 
> 
> ![Image 177: \displaystyle  \nu^-(n) \leq 1_{n \not \in\bigcup_{p|P} E_p}(n)](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle++%5Cnu%5E-%28n%29+%5Cleq+1_%7Bn+%5Cnot+%5Cin%5Cbigcup_%7Bp%7CP%7D+E_p%7D%28n%29&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)
> 
> 
> for all ![Image 178: {n \in {\bf Z}}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bn+%5Cin+%7B%5Cbf+Z%7D%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002). Thus for instance ![Image 179: {1}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B1%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) and ![Image 180: {0}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B0%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) are (trivially) upper bound sieves and lower bound sieves respectively.

_Proof:_ We prove part (i) only, and leave part (ii) as an exercise. Let ![Image 181: {A}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BA%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) be the supremal value of the quantity [(1)](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/#fep) given the constraints in Problem [3](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/#sivp), and let ![Image 182: {B}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BB%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) be the infimal value of ![Image 183: {\sum_{d \in {\mathcal D}} \lambda^+_d X_d}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Csum_%7Bd+%5Cin+%7B%5Cmathcal+D%7D%7D+%5Clambda%5E%2B_d+X_d%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002). We need to show that ![Image 184: {A=B}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BA%3DB%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002).

We first establish the easy inequality ![Image 185: {A \leq B}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BA+%5Cleq+B%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002). If the sequence ![Image 186: {a_n}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Ba_n%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) obeys the constraints in Problem [3](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/#sivp), and ![Image 187: {\nu^+ = \sum_{d \in {\mathcal D}} \lambda^+_d 1_{E_d}}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Cnu%5E%2B+%3D+%5Csum_%7Bd+%5Cin+%7B%5Cmathcal+D%7D%7D+%5Clambda%5E%2B_d+1_%7BE_d%7D%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) is an upper bound sieve, then

![Image 188: \displaystyle  \sum_n \nu^+(n) a_n = \sum_{d \in {\mathcal D}} \lambda^+_d X_d](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle++%5Csum_n+%5Cnu%5E%2B%28n%29+a_n+%3D+%5Csum_%7Bd+%5Cin+%7B%5Cmathcal+D%7D%7D+%5Clambda%5E%2B_d+X_d&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)

and hence (by the non-negativity of ![Image 189: {\nu^+}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Cnu%5E%2B%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) and ![Image 190: {a_n}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Ba_n%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002))

![Image 191: \displaystyle  \sum_{n \not \in \bigcup_{p|P} E_p} a_n \leq \sum_{d \in {\mathcal D}} \lambda^+_d X_d;](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle++%5Csum_%7Bn+%5Cnot+%5Cin+%5Cbigcup_%7Bp%7CP%7D+E_p%7D+a_n+%5Cleq+%5Csum_%7Bd+%5Cin+%7B%5Cmathcal+D%7D%7D+%5Clambda%5E%2B_d+X_d%3B&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)

taking suprema in ![Image 192: {f}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bf%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) and infima in ![Image 193: {\nu^+}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Cnu%5E%2B%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) we conclude that ![Image 194: {A \leq B}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BA+%5Cleq+B%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002).

Now suppose for contradiction that ![Image 195: {A<B}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BA%3CB%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002), thus ![Image 196: {A < C < B}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BA+%3C+C+%3C+B%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) for some real number ![Image 197: {C}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BC%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002). We will argue using the [hyperplane separation theorem](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Separating_hyperplane_theorem); one can also proceed using one of the other duality results mentioned above. (See [this previous blog post](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2007/11/30/the-hahn-banach-theorem-mengers-theorem-and-hellys-theorem/) for some discussion of the connections between these various forms of linear duality.) Consider the affine functional

![Image 198: \displaystyle  \rho_0: (a_n)_{n \in{\bf Z}} \mapsto C - \sum_{n \not \in \bigcup_{p|P} E_p} a_n. ](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle++%5Crho_0%3A+%28a_n%29_%7Bn+%5Cin%7B%5Cbf+Z%7D%7D+%5Cmapsto+C+-+%5Csum_%7Bn+%5Cnot+%5Cin+%5Cbigcup_%7Bp%7CP%7D+E_p%7D+a_n.+&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)

on the vector space of finitely supported sequences ![Image 199: {(a_n)_{n \in {\bf Z}}}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%28a_n%29_%7Bn+%5Cin+%7B%5Cbf+Z%7D%7D%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) of reals. On the one hand, since ![Image 200: {C > A}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BC+%3E+A%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002), this functional is positive for every sequence ![Image 201: {(a_n)_{n \in{\bf Z}}}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%28a_n%29_%7Bn+%5Cin%7B%5Cbf+Z%7D%7D%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) obeying the constraints in Problem [3](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/#sivp). Next, let ![Image 202: {K}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BK%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) be the space of affine functionals ![Image 203: {\rho}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Crho%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) of the form

![Image 204: \displaystyle  \rho: (a_n)_{n \in {\bf Z}} \mapsto -\sum_{d \in {\mathcal D}} \lambda^+_d ( \sum_{n \in E_d} a_n - X_d ) + \sum_n a_n \nu(n) + X](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle++%5Crho%3A+%28a_n%29_%7Bn+%5Cin+%7B%5Cbf+Z%7D%7D+%5Cmapsto+-%5Csum_%7Bd+%5Cin+%7B%5Cmathcal+D%7D%7D+%5Clambda%5E%2B_d+%28+%5Csum_%7Bn+%5Cin+E_d%7D+a_n+-+X_d+%29+%2B+%5Csum_n+a_n+%5Cnu%28n%29+%2B+X&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)

for some real numbers ![Image 205: {\lambda^+_d \in {\bf R}}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Clambda%5E%2B_d+%5Cin+%7B%5Cbf+R%7D%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002), some non-negative function ![Image 206: {\nu: {\bf Z} \rightarrow {\bf R}^+}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Cnu%3A+%7B%5Cbf+Z%7D+%5Crightarrow+%7B%5Cbf+R%7D%5E%2B%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) which is a finite linear combination of the ![Image 207: {1_{E_d}}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B1_%7BE_d%7D%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) for ![Image 208: {d|P}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bd%7CP%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002), and some non-negative ![Image 209: {X}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BX%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002). This is a closed convex cone in a finite-dimensional vector space ![Image 210: {V}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BV%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002); note also that ![Image 211: {\rho_0}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Crho_0%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) lies in ![Image 212: {V}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BV%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002). Suppose first that ![Image 213: {\rho_0 \in K}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Crho_0+%5Cin+K%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002), thus we have a representation of the form

![Image 214: \displaystyle C - \sum_{n \not \in \bigcup_{p|P} E_p} a_n = -\sum_{d \in {\mathcal D}} \lambda^+_d ( \sum_{n \in E_d} a_n - X_d ) + \sum_n a_n \nu(n) + X](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle+C+-+%5Csum_%7Bn+%5Cnot+%5Cin+%5Cbigcup_%7Bp%7CP%7D+E_p%7D+a_n+%3D+-%5Csum_%7Bd+%5Cin+%7B%5Cmathcal+D%7D%7D+%5Clambda%5E%2B_d+%28+%5Csum_%7Bn+%5Cin+E_d%7D+a_n+-+X_d+%29+%2B+%5Csum_n+a_n+%5Cnu%28n%29+%2B+X&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)

for any finitely supported sequence ![Image 215: {(a_n)_{n \in {\bf Z}}}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%28a_n%29_%7Bn+%5Cin+%7B%5Cbf+Z%7D%7D%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002). Comparing coefficients, we conclude that

![Image 216: \displaystyle  \sum_{d \in {\mathcal D}} \lambda^+_d 1_{E_d}(n) \geq 1_{n \not \in \bigcup_{p|P} E_p}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle++%5Csum_%7Bd+%5Cin+%7B%5Cmathcal+D%7D%7D+%5Clambda%5E%2B_d+1_%7BE_d%7D%28n%29+%5Cgeq+1_%7Bn+%5Cnot+%5Cin+%5Cbigcup_%7Bp%7CP%7D+E_p%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)

for any ![Image 217: {n}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bn%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) (i.e., ![Image 218: {\sum_{d \in {\mathcal D}} \lambda^+_d 1_{E_d}}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Csum_%7Bd+%5Cin+%7B%5Cmathcal+D%7D%7D+%5Clambda%5E%2B_d+1_%7BE_d%7D%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) is an upper bound sieve), and also

![Image 219: \displaystyle  C \geq \sum_{d \in {\mathcal D}} \lambda^+_d X_d,](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle++C+%5Cgeq+%5Csum_%7Bd+%5Cin+%7B%5Cmathcal+D%7D%7D+%5Clambda%5E%2B_d+X_d%2C&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)

and thus ![Image 220: {C \geq B}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BC+%5Cgeq+B%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002), a contradiction. Thus ![Image 221: {\rho_0}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Crho_0%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) lies outside of ![Image 222: {K}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BK%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002). But then by the hyperplane separation theorem, we can find an affine functional ![Image 223: {\iota: V \rightarrow {\bf R}}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Ciota%3A+V+%5Crightarrow+%7B%5Cbf+R%7D%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) on ![Image 224: {V}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BV%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) that is non-negative on ![Image 225: {K}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BK%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) and negative on ![Image 226: {\rho_0}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Crho_0%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002). By duality, such an affine functional takes the form ![Image 227: {\iota: \rho \mapsto \rho((b_n)_{n \in {\bf Z}}) + c}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Ciota%3A+%5Crho+%5Cmapsto+%5Crho%28%28b_n%29_%7Bn+%5Cin+%7B%5Cbf+Z%7D%7D%29+%2B+c%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) for some finitely supported sequence ![Image 228: {(b_n)_{n \in {\bf Z}}}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%28b_n%29_%7Bn+%5Cin+%7B%5Cbf+Z%7D%7D%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) and ![Image 229: {c \in {\bf R}}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bc+%5Cin+%7B%5Cbf+R%7D%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) (indeed, ![Image 230: {(b_n)_{n \in {\bf Z}}}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%28b_n%29_%7Bn+%5Cin+%7B%5Cbf+Z%7D%7D%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) can be supported on a finite set consisting of a single representative for each atom of the finite ![Image 231: {\sigma}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Csigma%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)-algebra generated by the ![Image 232: {E_p}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BE_p%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)). Since ![Image 233: {\iota}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Ciota%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) is non-negative on the cone ![Image 234: {K}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BK%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002), we see (on testing against multiples of the functionals ![Image 235: {(a_n)_{n \in {\bf Z}} \mapsto \sum_{n \in E_d} a_n - X_d}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%28a_n%29_%7Bn+%5Cin+%7B%5Cbf+Z%7D%7D+%5Cmapsto+%5Csum_%7Bn+%5Cin+E_d%7D+a_n+-+X_d%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) or ![Image 236: {(a_n)_{n \in {\bf Z}} \mapsto a_n}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%28a_n%29_%7Bn+%5Cin+%7B%5Cbf+Z%7D%7D+%5Cmapsto+a_n%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)) that the ![Image 237: {b_n}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bb_n%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) and ![Image 238: {c}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bc%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) are non-negative, and that ![Image 239: {\sum_{n \in E_d} b_n - X_d = 0}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Csum_%7Bn+%5Cin+E_d%7D+b_n+-+X_d+%3D+0%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) for all ![Image 240: {d \in {\mathcal D}}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bd+%5Cin+%7B%5Cmathcal+D%7D%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002); thus ![Image 241: {(b_n)_{n \in {\bf Z}}}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%28b_n%29_%7Bn+%5Cin+%7B%5Cbf+Z%7D%7D%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) is feasible for Problem [3](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/#sivp). Since ![Image 242: {\iota}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Ciota%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) is negative on ![Image 243: {\rho_0}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Crho_0%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002), we see that

![Image 244: \displaystyle  \sum_{n \not \in \bigcup_{p|P} E_p} b_n \geq C ](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle++%5Csum_%7Bn+%5Cnot+%5Cin+%5Cbigcup_%7Bp%7CP%7D+E_p%7D+b_n+%5Cgeq+C+&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)

and thus ![Image 245: {A \geq C}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BA+%5Cgeq+C%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002), giving the desired contradiction. ![Image 246: \Box](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5CBox&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)

> **Exercise 6** Prove part (ii) of the above theorem.

> **Exercise 7** Show that the infima and suprema in the above theorem are actually attained (so one can replace “infimal” and “supremal” by “minimal” and “maximal” if desired).

> **Exercise 8**[](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/) What are the optimal upper and lower bound sieves for Exercise [4](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/#easy)?

In the case when ![Image 247: {{\mathcal D}}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%7B%5Cmathcal+D%7D%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) consists of _all_ the divisors of ![Image 248: {P}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BP%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002), we see that the Legendre sieve ![Image 249: {\sum_{d|P} \mu(d) 1_{E_d}}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Csum_%7Bd%7CP%7D+%5Cmu%28d%29+1_%7BE_d%7D%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) is both the optimal upper bound sieve and the optimal lower bound sieve, regardless of what the quantities ![Image 250: {X_d}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BX_d%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) are. However, in most cases of interest, ![Image 251: {{\mathcal D}}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%7B%5Cmathcal+D%7D%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) will only be some strict subset of the divisors of ![Image 252: {P}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BP%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002), and there will be a gap between the optimal upper and lower bounds.

Observe that a sequence ![Image 253: {(\lambda^+_d)_{d \in {\mathcal D}}}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%28%5Clambda%5E%2B_d%29_%7Bd+%5Cin+%7B%5Cmathcal+D%7D%7D%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) of real numbers will form an upper bound sieve ![Image 254: {\sum_d \lambda^+_d 1_{E_d}}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Csum_d+%5Clambda%5E%2B_d+1_%7BE_d%7D%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) if one has the inequalities

![Image 255: \displaystyle  \lambda^+_1 \geq 1](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle++%5Clambda%5E%2B_1+%5Cgeq+1&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)

and

![Image 256: \displaystyle  \sum_{d|n} \lambda^+_d \geq 0](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle++%5Csum_%7Bd%7Cn%7D+%5Clambda%5E%2B_d+%5Cgeq+0&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)

for all ![Image 257: {n|P}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bn%7CP%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002); we will refer to such sequences as _upper bound sieve coefficients_. (Conversely, if the sets ![Image 258: {E_p}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BE_p%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) are in “general position” in the sense that every set of the form ![Image 259: {\bigcap_{p|n} E_p \backslash \bigcup_{p|P; p\not | n} E_p}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Cbigcap_%7Bp%7Cn%7D+E_p+%5Cbackslash+%5Cbigcup_%7Bp%7CP%3B+p%5Cnot+%7C+n%7D+E_p%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) for ![Image 260: {n|P}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bn%7CP%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) is non-empty, we see that every upper bound sieve arises from a sequence of upper bound sieve coefficients.) Similarly, a sequence ![Image 261: {(\lambda^-_d)_{d \in {\mathcal D}}}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%28%5Clambda%5E-_d%29_%7Bd+%5Cin+%7B%5Cmathcal+D%7D%7D%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) of real numbers will form a lower bound sieve ![Image 262: {\sum_d \lambda^-_d 1_{E_d}}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Csum_d+%5Clambda%5E-_d+1_%7BE_d%7D%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) if one has the inequalities

![Image 263: \displaystyle  \lambda^-_1 \leq 1](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle++%5Clambda%5E-_1+%5Cleq+1&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)

and

![Image 264: \displaystyle  \sum_{d|n} \lambda^-_d \leq 0](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle++%5Csum_%7Bd%7Cn%7D+%5Clambda%5E-_d+%5Cleq+0&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)

for all ![Image 265: {n|P}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bn%7CP%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) with ![Image 266: {n>1}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bn%3E1%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002); we will refer to such sequences as _lower bound sieve coefficients_.

> **Exercise 9 (Brun pure sieve)**[](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/) Let ![Image 267: {P}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BP%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) be a squarefree number, and ![Image 268: {k}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bk%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) a non-negative integer. Show that the sequence ![Image 269: {(\lambda_d)_{d | P}}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%28%5Clambda_d%29_%7Bd+%7C+P%7D%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) defined by
> 
> 
> ![Image 270: \displaystyle  \lambda_d := 1_{\omega(d) \leq k} \mu(d),](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle++%5Clambda_d+%3A%3D+1_%7B%5Comega%28d%29+%5Cleq+k%7D+%5Cmu%28d%29%2C&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)
> 
> 
> where (in contrast with the rest of this set of notes) ![Image 271: {\omega(d)}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Comega%28d%29%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) denotes the number of prime factors of ![Image 272: {d}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bd%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002), is a sequence of upper bound sieve coefficients for even ![Image 273: {k}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bk%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002), and a sequence of lower bound sieve coefficients for odd ![Image 274: {k}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bk%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002). Deduce the [Bonferroni inequalities](http://mathworld.wolfram.com/BonferroniInequalities.html)[](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/)
> 
> [![Image 275: \displaystyle \sum_{n \in {\bf Z}} a_n 1_{n \not \in \bigcup_{p | P} E_p} \leq \sum_{d|P: \omega(d) \leq k} \mu(d) X_d \ \ \ \ \ (11)](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle++%5Csum_%7Bn+%5Cin+%7B%5Cbf+Z%7D%7D+a_n+1_%7Bn+%5Cnot+%5Cin+%5Cbigcup_%7Bp+%7C+P%7D+E_p%7D+%5Cleq+%5Csum_%7Bd%7CP%3A+%5Comega%28d%29+%5Cleq+k%7D+%5Cmu%28d%29+X_d+%5C+%5C+%5C+%5C+%5C+%2811%29&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/)
> [](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/) when ![Image 276: {k}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bk%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) is even, and [](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/)
> 
> [![Image 277: \displaystyle \sum_{n \in {\bf Z}} a_n 1_{n \not \in \bigcup_{p | P} E_p} \geq \sum_{d|P: \omega(d) \leq k} \mu(d) X_d \ \ \ \ \ (12)](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle++%5Csum_%7Bn+%5Cin+%7B%5Cbf+Z%7D%7D+a_n+1_%7Bn+%5Cnot+%5Cin+%5Cbigcup_%7Bp+%7C+P%7D+E_p%7D+%5Cgeq+%5Csum_%7Bd%7CP%3A+%5Comega%28d%29+%5Cleq+k%7D+%5Cmu%28d%29+X_d+%5C+%5C+%5C+%5C+%5C+%2812%29&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/)
> [](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/) when ![Image 278: {k}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bk%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) is odd, whenever one is in the situation of Problem [3](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/#sivp) (and ![Image 279: {{\mathcal D}}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%7B%5Cmathcal+D%7D%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) contains all ![Image 280: {d|P}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bd%7CP%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) with ![Image 281: {\omega(d) \leq k}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Comega%28d%29+%5Cleq+k%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)). The resulting upper and lower bound sieves are sometimes known as _Brun pure sieves_. The Legendre sieve can be viewed as the limiting case when ![Image 282: {k \geq \omega(P)}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bk+%5Cgeq+%5Comega%28P%29%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002).

In many applications the sums ![Image 283: {X_d}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BX_d%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) in [(9)](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/#xd-sum) take the form [](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/)

[![Image 284: \displaystyle \sum_{n \in E_d} a_n = g(d) X + r_d \ \ \ \ \ (13)](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle++%5Csum_%7Bn+%5Cin+E_d%7D+a_n+%3D+g%28d%29+X+%2B+r_d+%5C+%5C+%5C+%5C+%5C+%2813%29&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/)
[](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/) for some quantity ![Image 285: {X}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BX%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) independent of ![Image 286: {d}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bd%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002), some multiplicative function ![Image 287: {g}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bg%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) with ![Image 288: {0 \leq g(p) \leq 1}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B0+%5Cleq+g%28p%29+%5Cleq+1%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002), and some remainder term ![Image 289: {r_d}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Br_d%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) whose effect is expected to be negligible on average if ![Image 290: {d}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bd%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) is restricted to be small, e.g. less than a threshold ![Image 291: {D}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BD%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002); note for instance that [(5)](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/#sam) is of this form if ![Image 292: {D \leq x^{1-\varepsilon}}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BD+%5Cleq+x%5E%7B1-%5Cvarepsilon%7D%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) for some fixed ![Image 293: {\varepsilon>0}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Cvarepsilon%3E0%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) (note from the divisor bound, Lemma 23 of [Notes 1](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2014/11/23/254a-notes-1-elementary-multiplicative-number-theory/), that ![Image 294: {\prod_{p|d} \omega(p) \ll x^{o(1)}}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Cprod_%7Bp%7Cd%7D+%5Comega%28p%29+%5Cll+x%5E%7Bo%281%29%7D%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) if ![Image 295: {d \ll x^{O(1)}}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bd+%5Cll+x%5E%7BO%281%29%7D%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)). We are thus led to the following idealisation of the sieving problem, in which the remainder terms ![Image 296: {r_d}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Br_d%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) are ignored:

> **Problem 10 (Idealised sieving)**[](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/) Let ![Image 297: {z, D \geq 1}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bz%2C+D+%5Cgeq+1%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) (we refer to ![Image 298: {z}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bz%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) as the _sifting level_ and ![Image 299: {D}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BD%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) as the _level of distribution_), let ![Image 300: {g}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bg%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) be a multiplicative function with ![Image 301: {0 \leq g(p) \leq 1}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B0+%5Cleq+g%28p%29+%5Cleq+1%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002), and let ![Image 302: {{\mathcal D} := \{ d|P(z): d \leq D \}}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%7B%5Cmathcal+D%7D+%3A%3D+%5C%7B+d%7CP%28z%29%3A+d+%5Cleq+D+%5C%7D%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002). How small can one make the quantity [](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/)
> 
> [![Image 303: \displaystyle \sum_{d \in {\mathcal D}} \lambda^+_d g(d) \ \ \ \ \ (14)](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle++%5Csum_%7Bd+%5Cin+%7B%5Cmathcal+D%7D%7D+%5Clambda%5E%2B_d+g%28d%29+%5C+%5C+%5C+%5C+%5C+%2814%29&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/)
> [](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/) for a sequence ![Image 304: {(\lambda^+_d)_{d \in {\mathcal D}}}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%28%5Clambda%5E%2B_d%29_%7Bd+%5Cin+%7B%5Cmathcal+D%7D%7D%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) of upper bound sieve coefficients, and how large can one make the quantity [](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/)
> 
> [![Image 305: \displaystyle \sum_{d \in {\mathcal D}} \lambda^-_d g(d) \ \ \ \ \ (15)](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle++%5Csum_%7Bd+%5Cin+%7B%5Cmathcal+D%7D%7D+%5Clambda%5E-_d+g%28d%29+%5C+%5C+%5C+%5C+%5C+%2815%29&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/)
> [](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/) for a sequence ![Image 306: {(\lambda^-_d)_{d \in {\mathcal D}}}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%28%5Clambda%5E-_d%29_%7Bd+%5Cin+%7B%5Cmathcal+D%7D%7D%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) of lower bound sieve coefficients?

Thus, for instance, the trivial upper bound sieve ![Image 307: {\lambda^+_d := 1_{d=1}}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Clambda%5E%2B_d+%3A%3D+1_%7Bd%3D1%7D%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) and the trivial lower bound sieve ![Image 308: {\lambda^-_d := 0}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Clambda%5E-_d+%3A%3D+0%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) show that [(14)](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/#dplus) can equal ![Image 309: {1}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B1%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) and [(15)](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/#dminus) can equal ![Image 310: {0}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B0%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002). Of course, one hopes to do better than these trivial bounds in many situations; usually one can improve the upper bound quite substantially, but improving the lower bound is significantly more difficult, particularly when ![Image 311: {z}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bz%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) is large compared with ![Image 312: {D}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BD%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002).

If the remainder terms ![Image 313: {r_d}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Br_d%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) in [(13)](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/#road) are indeed negligible on average for ![Image 314: {d \leq D}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bd+%5Cleq+D%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002), then one expects the upper and lower bounds in Problem [3](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/#sivp) to essentially be the optimal bounds in [(14)](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/#dplus) and [(15)](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/#dminus) respectively, multiplied by the normalisation factor ![Image 315: {X}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BX%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002). Thus Problem [10](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/#ideal) serves as a good model problem for Problem [3](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/#sivp), in which all the arithmetic content of the original sieving problem has been abstracted into two parameters ![Image 316: {z,D}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bz%2CD%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) and a multiplicative function ![Image 317: {g}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bg%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002). In many applications, ![Image 318: {g(p)}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bg%28p%29%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) will be approximately ![Image 319: {\kappa/p}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Ckappa%2Fp%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) on the average for some fixed ![Image 320: {\kappa>0}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Ckappa%3E0%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002), known as the _sieve dimension_; for instance, in the twin prime sieving problem discussed above, the sieve dimension is ![Image 321: {2}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B2%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002). The larger one makes the level of distribution ![Image 322: {D}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BD%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) compared to ![Image 323: {z}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bz%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002), the more choices one has for the upper and lower bound sieves; it is thus of interest to obtain equidistribution estimates such as [(13)](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/#road) for ![Image 324: {d}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bd%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) as large as possible. When the sequence ![Image 325: {a_d}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Ba_d%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) is of arithmetic origin (for instance, if it is the von Mangoldt function ![Image 326: {\Lambda}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5CLambda%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)), then estimates such as the Bombieri-Vinogradov theorem, Theorem 17 from [Notes 3](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/10/254a-notes-3-the-large-sieve-and-the-bombieri-vinogradov-theorem/), turn out to be particularly useful in this regard; in other contexts, the required equidistribution estimates might come from other sources, such as homogeneous dynamics, or the theory of expander graphs (the latter arises in the recent theory of the _affine sieve_, discussed in [this previous blog post](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2012/03/01/254b-notes-7-sieving-and-expanders/)). However, the sieve-theoretic tools developed in this post are not particularly sensitive to _how_ a certain level of distribution is attained, and are generally content to use sieve axioms such as [(13)](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/#road) as “black boxes”.

In some applications one needs to modify Problem [10](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/#ideal) in various technical ways (e.g. in altering the product ![Image 327: {P(z)}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BP%28z%29%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002), the set ![Image 328: {{\mathcal D}}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%7B%5Cmathcal+D%7D%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002), or the definition of an upper or lower sieve coefficient sequence), but to simplify the exposition we will focus on the above problem without such alterations.

As the exercise below (or the heuristic [(7)](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/#heuristic)) suggests, the “natural” size of [(14)](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/#dplus) and [(15)](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/#dminus) is given by the quantity ![Image 329: {V(z) := \prod_{p < z} (1 - g(p))}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BV%28z%29+%3A%3D+%5Cprod_%7Bp+%3C+z%7D+%281+-+g%28p%29%29%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) (so that the natural size for Problem [3](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/#sivp) is ![Image 330: {V(z) X}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BV%28z%29+X%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)):

> **Exercise 11** Let ![Image 331: {z,D,g}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bz%2CD%2Cg%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) be as in Problem [10](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/#ideal), and set ![Image 332: {V(z) := \prod_{p \leq z} (1 - g(p))}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BV%28z%29+%3A%3D+%5Cprod_%7Bp+%5Cleq+z%7D+%281+-+g%28p%29%29%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002).

The problem of finding good sequences of upper and lower bound sieve coefficients in order to solve problems such as Problem [10](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/#ideal) is one of the core objectives of sieve theory, and has been intensively studied. This is more of an optimisation problem rather than a genuinely number theoretic problem; however, the optimisation problem is sufficiently complicated that it has not been solved exactly or even asymptotically, except in a few special cases. (It can be reduced to a optimisation problem involving multilinear integrals of certain unknown functions of several variables, but this problem is rather difficult to analyse further; see [these lecture notes of Selberg](http://www.ams.org/mathscinet-getitem?mr=1295844) for further discussion.) But while we do not yet have a definitive solution to this problem in general, we do have a number of good general-purpose upper and lower bound sieve coefficients that give fairly good values for [(14)](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/#dplus), [(15)](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/#dminus), often coming within a constant factor of the idealised value ![Image 333: {V(z)}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BV%28z%29%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002), and which work well for sifting levels ![Image 334: {z}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bz%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) as large as a small power of the level of distribution ![Image 335: {D}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BD%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002). Unfortunately, we also know of an important limitation to the sieve, known as the _parity problem_, that prevents one from taking ![Image 336: {z}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bz%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) as large as ![Image 337: {D^{1/2}}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BD%5E%7B1%2F2%7D%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) while still obtaining non-trivial lower bounds; as a consequence, sieve theory is not able, on its own, to sift out primes for such purposes as establishing the twin prime conjecture. However, it is still possible to use these sieves, in conjunction with additional tools, to produce various types of primes or prime patterns in some cases; examples of this include [the theorem of Ben Green and myself](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Green%E2%80%93Tao_theorem) in which an upper bound sieve is used to demonstrate the existence of primes in arbitrarily long arithmetic progressions, or the more recent [theorem of Zhang](http://www.ams.org/mathscinet-getitem?mr=2552109) in which an upper bound sieve (among other things) was used to demonstrate the existence of infinitely many pairs of primes whose difference was bounded. In such arguments, the upper bound sieve was used not so much to count the primes or prime patterns directly, but to serve instead as a sort of “container” to efficiently envelop such prime patterns; when used in such a manner, the upper bound sieves are sometimes known as _enveloping sieves_. If the original sequence was supported on primes, then the enveloping sieve can be viewed as a “smoothed out indicator function” that is concentrated on _almost primes_, which in this context refers to numbers with no small prime factors.

In a somewhat different direction, it can be possible in some cases to break the parity barrier by assuming additional equidistribution axioms on the sequence ![Image 338: {a_n}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Ba_n%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) than just [(13)](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/#road), in particular controlling certain _bilinear_ sums involving ![Image 339: {a_{nm}}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Ba_%7Bnm%7D%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) rather than just _linear_ sums of the ![Image 340: {a_n}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Ba_n%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002). This approach was in particular pursued by Friedlander and Iwaniec, leading to [their theorem](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Friedlander%E2%80%93Iwaniec_theorem) that there are infinitely many primes of the form ![Image 341: {n^2+m^4}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bn%5E2%2Bm%5E4%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002).

The study of sieves is an immense topic; see for instance the [recent 527-page text by Friedlander and Iwaniec](http://www.ams.org/mathscinet-getitem?mr=2647984). We will limit attention to two sieves which give good general-purpose results, if not necessarily the most optimal ones:

*   (i) The _beta sieve_ (or _Rosser-Iwaniec sieve_), which is a modification of the classical combinatorial sieve of Brun. (A collection of sieve coefficients ![Image 342: {\lambda_d^{\pm}}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Clambda_d%5E%7B%5Cpm%7D%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) is called _combinatorial_ if its coefficients lie in ![Image 343: {\{-1,0,+1\}}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5C%7B-1%2C0%2C%2B1%5C%7D%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002).) The beta sieve is a family of upper and lower bound combinatorial sieves, and are particularly useful for efficiently sieving out all primes up to a parameter ![Image 344: {z = x^{1/u}}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bz+%3D+x%5E%7B1%2Fu%7D%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) from a set of integers of size ![Image 345: {x}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bx%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002), in the regime where ![Image 346: {u}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bu%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) is moderately large, leading to what is sometimes known as the [fundamental lemma of sieve theory](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fundamental_lemma_of_sieve_theory). 
*   (ii) The _Selberg upper bound sieve_, which is a general-purpose sieve that can serve both as an upper bound sieve for classical sieving problems, as well as an enveloping sieve for sets such as the primes. (One can also convert the Selberg upper bound sieve into a lower bound sieve in a number of ways, but we will only touch upon this briefly.) A key advantage of the Selberg sieve is that, due to the “quadratic” nature of the sieve, the difficult optimisation problem in Problem [10](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/#ideal) is replaced with a much more tractable quadratic optimisation problem, which can often be solved for exactly. 

> **Remark 12** It is possible to compose two sieves together, for instance by using the observation that the product of two upper bound sieves is again an upper bound sieve, or that the product of an upper bound sieve and a lower bound sieve is a lower bound sieve. Such a composition of sieves is useful in some applications, for instance if one wants to apply the fundamental lemma as a “preliminary sieve” to sieve out small primes, but then use a more precise sieve like the Selberg sieve to sieve out medium primes. We will see an example of this in later notes, when we discuss the linear beta-sieve.

We will also briefly present the (arithmetic) _large sieve_, which gives a rather different approach to Problem [3](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/#sivp) in the case that each ![Image 347: {E_p}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BE_p%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) consists of some number (typically a large number) of residue classes modulo ![Image 348: {p}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bp%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002), and is powered by the (analytic) large sieve inequality of the preceding section. As an application of these methods, we will utilise the Selberg upper bound sieve as an enveloping sieve to establish Zhang’s theorem on bounded gaps between primes. Finally, we give an informal discussion of the _parity barrier_ which gives some heuristic limitations on what sieve theory is able to accomplish with regards to counting prime patters such as twin primes.

These notes are only an introduction to the vast topic of sieve theory; more detailed discussion can be found in the [Friedlander-Iwaniec text](http://www.ams.org/mathscinet-getitem?mr=2647984), in [these lecture notes of Selberg](http://www.ams.org/mathscinet-getitem?mr=1295844), and in many further texts.

**— 1. Combinatorial sieving and the fundamental lemma of sieve theory —**

We begin with a discusion of _combinatorial upper and lower bound sieves_, in which the sieve coefficients ![Image 349: {\lambda^\pm_d}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Clambda%5E%5Cpm_d%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) take values in ![Image 350: {\{-1,0,+1\}}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5C%7B-1%2C0%2C%2B1%5C%7D%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002).
