These sieves, first introduced by Brun, can be viewed as truncations of the Legendre sieve (which corresponds to the choice of coefficients ![Image 351: {\lambda^\pm_d = \mu(d)}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Clambda%5E%5Cpm_d+%3D+%5Cmu%28d%29%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) for all ![Image 352: {d|P(z)}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bd%7CP%28z%29%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)). The Legendre sieve, and more generally the Brun pure sieves in Exercise [9](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/#bon), give basic examples of combinatorial sieves.

We loosely follow the [discussion of Friedlander and Iwaniec](http://www.ams.org/mathscinet-getitem?mr=2647984). The combinatorial sieves may be motivated by observing the _Buchstab identity_[](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/)

[![Image 353: \displaystyle 1_{n \not \in \bigcup_{p < z} E_p} = 1 - \sum_{p_1 < z} 1_{E_{p_1}}(n) 1_{n \not \in \bigcup_{p < p_1} E_p} \ \ \ \ \ (16)](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle++1_%7Bn+%5Cnot+%5Cin+%5Cbigcup_%7Bp+%3C+z%7D+E_p%7D+%3D+1+-+%5Csum_%7Bp_1+%3C+z%7D+1_%7BE_%7Bp_1%7D%7D%28n%29+1_%7Bn+%5Cnot+%5Cin+%5Cbigcup_%7Bp+%3C+p_1%7D+E_p%7D+%5C+%5C+%5C+%5C+%5C+%2816%29&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/)
[](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/) for any ![Image 354: {n \in {\bf Z}}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bn+%5Cin+%7B%5Cbf+Z%7D%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) and any collection of sets ![Image 355: {E_p \subset {\bf Z}}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BE_p+%5Csubset+%7B%5Cbf+Z%7D%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) for ![Image 356: {p < z}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bp+%3C+z%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002). This identity reflects the basic fact that if ![Image 357: {n}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bn%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) does lie in ![Image 358: {\bigcup_{p < z} E_p}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Cbigcup_%7Bp+%3C+z%7D+E_p%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002), then there is a unique prime ![Image 359: {p_1 < z}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bp_1+%3C+z%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) such that ![Image 360: {n}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bn%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) lies in ![Image 361: {E_{p_1}}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BE_%7Bp_1%7D%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002), but does not lie in ![Image 362: {E_p}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BE_p%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) for any ![Image 363: {p < p_1}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bp+%3C+p_1%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002).

There is an analogous identity for the function ![Image 364: {g}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bg%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002):

> **Exercise 13** For any arithmetic function ![Image 365: {g}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bg%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002), show that
> 
> 
> ![Image 366: \displaystyle  \prod_{p<z} (1-g(p)) = 1 - \sum_{p_1 < z} g(p_1) \prod_{p<p_1} (1-g(p)).](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle++%5Cprod_%7Bp%3Cz%7D+%281-g%28p%29%29+%3D+1+-+%5Csum_%7Bp_1+%3C+z%7D+g%28p_1%29+%5Cprod_%7Bp%3Cp_1%7D+%281-g%28p%29%29.&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)
> 
> 
> How is this identity related to [(16)](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/#geo)?

The Buchstab identity [(16)](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/#geo) allows one to convert upper bound sieves into lower bound sieves and vice versa. Indeed, if ![Image 367: {\sum_{d|P(p_1)} \lambda^{-,p_1}_d 1_{E_d}}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Csum_%7Bd%7CP%28p_1%29%7D+%5Clambda%5E%7B-%2Cp_1%7D_d+1_%7BE_d%7D%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) is a family of lower bound sieves for each ![Image 368: {p_1 < z}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bp_1+%3C+z%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002), then [(16)](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/#geo) tells us that

![Image 369: \displaystyle  1 - \sum_{p_1 < z} \sum_{d|P(p_1)} \lambda^{-,p_1}_d 1_{E_{p_1 d}} ](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle++1+-+%5Csum_%7Bp_1+%3C+z%7D+%5Csum_%7Bd%7CP%28p_1%29%7D+%5Clambda%5E%7B-%2Cp_1%7D_d+1_%7BE_%7Bp_1+d%7D%7D+&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)

is an upper bound sieve; similarly, if ![Image 370: {\sum_{d|P(p_1)} \lambda^{+,p_1}_d 1_{E_d}}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Csum_%7Bd%7CP%28p_1%29%7D+%5Clambda%5E%7B%2B%2Cp_1%7D_d+1_%7BE_d%7D%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) is a family of upper bound sieves for each ![Image 371: {p_1 < z}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bp_1+%3C+z%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002), then

![Image 372: \displaystyle  1 - \sum_{p_1 < z} \sum_{d|P(p_1)} \lambda^{+,p_1}_d 1_{E_{p_1 d}} ](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle++1+-+%5Csum_%7Bp_1+%3C+z%7D+%5Csum_%7Bd%7CP%28p_1%29%7D+%5Clambda%5E%7B%2B%2Cp_1%7D_d+1_%7BE_%7Bp_1+d%7D%7D+&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)

is a lower bound sieve. In terms of sieve coefficients, we see that if ![Image 373: {(\lambda^{-,p_1}_d)_{d | P(p_1)}}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%28%5Clambda%5E%7B-%2Cp_1%7D_d%29_%7Bd+%7C+P%28p_1%29%7D%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) are lower bound sieve coefficients for each ![Image 374: {p_1 < z}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bp_1+%3C+z%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002), then

![Image 375: \displaystyle  \left( 1_{d=1} - 1_{d > 1} \lambda^{-,p^*(d)}_{d/p^*(d)} \right)_{d|P(z)}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle++%5Cleft%28+1_%7Bd%3D1%7D+-+1_%7Bd+%3E+1%7D+%5Clambda%5E%7B-%2Cp%5E%2A%28d%29%7D_%7Bd%2Fp%5E%2A%28d%29%7D+%5Cright%29_%7Bd%7CP%28z%29%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)

is a sequence of upper bound sieve coefficients (where ![Image 376: {p^*(d)}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bp%5E%2A%28d%29%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) denotes the largest prime factor of ![Image 377: {d}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bd%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)). Similarly, if ![Image 378: {(\lambda^{+,p_1}_d)_{d | P(p_1)}}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%28%5Clambda%5E%7B%2B%2Cp_1%7D_d%29_%7Bd+%7C+P%28p_1%29%7D%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) are upper bound sieve coefficients for each ![Image 379: {p_1 < z}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bp_1+%3C+z%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002), then

![Image 380: \displaystyle  \left( 1_{d=1} - 1_{d > 1} \lambda^{+,p^*(d)}_{d/p^*(d)} \right)_{d|P(z)}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle++%5Cleft%28+1_%7Bd%3D1%7D+-+1_%7Bd+%3E+1%7D+%5Clambda%5E%7B%2B%2Cp%5E%2A%28d%29%7D_%7Bd%2Fp%5E%2A%28d%29%7D+%5Cright%29_%7Bd%7CP%28z%29%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)

is a sequence of lower bound sieve coefficients.

One can iterate this procedure to produce an alternating sequence of upper and lower bound sieves. If one iterates out the Buchstab formula completely, one ends up back at the Legendre sieve. However, one can truncate this iteration by using the trivial lower bound sieve of ![Image 381: {0}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B0%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) for some of the primes ![Image 382: {p_1}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bp_1%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002). For instance, suppose one seeks an upper bound for ![Image 383: {1_{n \not \in \bigcup_{p < z} E_p}}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B1_%7Bn+%5Cnot+%5Cin+%5Cbigcup_%7Bp+%3C+z%7D+E_p%7D%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002). Applying [(16)](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/#geo), we only save some of the summands, say those ![Image 384: {p_1}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bp_1%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) which obey some predicate ![Image 385: {A_1(p_1)}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BA_1%28p_1%29%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) to be chosen later. For the remaining summands, we use the trivial lower bound sieve of ![Image 386: {0}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B0%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002), giving

![Image 387: \displaystyle  1_{n \not \in \bigcup_{p < z} E_p} \leq 1 - \sum_{p_1 < z: A_1(p_1)} 1_{E_{p_1}}(n) 1_{n \not \in \bigcup_{p < p_1} E_p}.](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle++1_%7Bn+%5Cnot+%5Cin+%5Cbigcup_%7Bp+%3C+z%7D+E_p%7D+%5Cleq+1+-+%5Csum_%7Bp_1+%3C+z%3A+A_1%28p_1%29%7D+1_%7BE_%7Bp_1%7D%7D%28n%29+1_%7Bn+%5Cnot+%5Cin+%5Cbigcup_%7Bp+%3C+p_1%7D+E_p%7D.&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)

For the surviving summands, we apply [(16)](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/#geo) again. With the sign change, the trivial lower bound sieve is not applicable, so we do not discard any further summands and arrive at

![Image 388: \displaystyle  1_{n \not \in \bigcup_{p < z} E_p} \leq 1 - \sum_{p_1 < z: A_1(p_1)} 1_{E_{p_1}}(n) ](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle++1_%7Bn+%5Cnot+%5Cin+%5Cbigcup_%7Bp+%3C+z%7D+E_p%7D+%5Cleq+1+-+%5Csum_%7Bp_1+%3C+z%3A+A_1%28p_1%29%7D+1_%7BE_%7Bp_1%7D%7D%28n%29+&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)

![Image 389: \displaystyle  + \sum_{p_2 < p_1 < z: A_1(p_1)} 1_{E_{p_1 p_2}}(n) 1_{n \not \in \bigcup_{p < p_2} E_p}.](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle++%2B+%5Csum_%7Bp_2+%3C+p_1+%3C+z%3A+A_1%28p_1%29%7D+1_%7BE_%7Bp_1+p_2%7D%7D%28n%29+1_%7Bn+%5Cnot+%5Cin+%5Cbigcup_%7Bp+%3C+p_2%7D+E_p%7D.&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)

For the summands in the second sum on the right, we apply [(16)](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/#geo) once again. We once again have a favourable sign and can use the trivial lower bound sieve of ![Image 390: {0}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B0%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) to discard some of the resulting summands, keeping only those triples ![Image 391: {p_1,p_2,p_3}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bp_1%2Cp_2%2Cp_3%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) that obey some predicate ![Image 392: {A_3(p_1,p_2,p_3)}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BA_3%28p_1%2Cp_2%2Cp_3%29%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002), giving

![Image 393: \displaystyle  1_{n \not \in \bigcup_{p < z} E_p} \leq 1 - \sum_{p_1 < z: A_1(p_1)} 1_{E_{p_1}}(n) ](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle++1_%7Bn+%5Cnot+%5Cin+%5Cbigcup_%7Bp+%3C+z%7D+E_p%7D+%5Cleq+1+-+%5Csum_%7Bp_1+%3C+z%3A+A_1%28p_1%29%7D+1_%7BE_%7Bp_1%7D%7D%28n%29+&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)

![Image 394: \displaystyle  + \sum_{p_2 < p_1 < z: A_1(p_1)} 1_{E_{p_1 p_2}}(n) ](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle++%2B+%5Csum_%7Bp_2+%3C+p_1+%3C+z%3A+A_1%28p_1%29%7D+1_%7BE_%7Bp_1+p_2%7D%7D%28n%29+&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)

![Image 395: \displaystyle  - \sum_{p_3 < p_2 < p_1 < z: A_1(p_1) \wedge A_3(p_1,p_2,p_3)} 1_{E_{p_1 p_2 p_3}}(n) 1_{n \not \in \bigcup_{p < p_3} E_p}.](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle++-+%5Csum_%7Bp_3+%3C+p_2+%3C+p_1+%3C+z%3A+A_1%28p_1%29+%5Cwedge+A_3%28p_1%2Cp_2%2Cp_3%29%7D+1_%7BE_%7Bp_1+p_2+p_3%7D%7D%28n%29+1_%7Bn+%5Cnot+%5Cin+%5Cbigcup_%7Bp+%3C+p_3%7D+E_p%7D.&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)

Since one cannot have an infinite descent of primes, this process will eventually terminate to produce an upper bound sieve. A similar argument (but with the signs reversed) gives a lower bound sieve. We formalise this discussion as follows:

> **Proposition 14 (General combinatorial sieve)**[](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/) Let ![Image 396: {z > 0}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bz+%3E+0%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002). For each natural number ![Image 397: {r}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Br%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002), let ![Image 398: {A_r(p_1,\dots,p_r)}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BA_r%28p_1%2C%5Cdots%2Cp_r%29%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) be a predicate pertaining to a sequence ![Image 399: {z > p_1 > \dots > p_r}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bz+%3E+p_1+%3E+%5Cdots+%3E+p_r%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) of ![Image 400: {r}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Br%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) decreasing primes, thus ![Image 401: {A_r(p_1,\dots,p_r)}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BA_r%28p_1%2C%5Cdots%2Cp_r%29%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) is either true or false for a given choice of such primes. Let ![Image 402: {{\mathcal D}_+}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%7B%5Cmathcal+D%7D_%2B%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) (resp. ![Image 403: {{\mathcal D}_-}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%7B%5Cmathcal+D%7D_-%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)) denote the set of ![Image 404: {d|P(z)}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bd%7CP%28z%29%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) which, when factored as ![Image 405: {d = p_1 \dots p_m}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bd+%3D+p_1+%5Cdots+p_m%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) for ![Image 406: {z > p_1 > \dots > p_m}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bz+%3E+p_1+%3E+%5Cdots+%3E+p_m%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002), are such that ![Image 407: {A_r(p_1,\dots,p_r)}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BA_r%28p_1%2C%5Cdots%2Cp_r%29%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) holds for all odd (resp. even) ![Image 408: {1 \leq r \leq m}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B1+%5Cleq+r+%5Cleq+m%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002). Thus
> 
> 
> ![Image 409: \displaystyle  {\mathcal D}_+ := \{ p_1 < z: A_1(p_1) \} \cup \{ p_1 p_2: p_2 < p_1 < z; A_1(p_1)\}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle++%7B%5Cmathcal+D%7D_%2B+%3A%3D+%5C%7B+p_1+%3C+z%3A+A_1%28p_1%29+%5C%7D+%5Ccup+%5C%7B+p_1+p_2%3A+p_2+%3C+p_1+%3C+z%3B+A_1%28p_1%29%5C%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)
> 
> 
> ![Image 410: \displaystyle  \cup \{p_1 p_2 p_3: p_3 < p_2 < p_1 < z; A_1(p_1) \wedge A_3(p_1,p_2,p_3) \}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle++%5Ccup+%5C%7Bp_1+p_2+p_3%3A+p_3+%3C+p_2+%3C+p_1+%3C+z%3B+A_1%28p_1%29+%5Cwedge+A_3%28p_1%2Cp_2%2Cp_3%29+%5C%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)
> 
> 
> ![Image 411: \displaystyle  \cup \{p_1 p_2 p_3 p_4: p_4 < p_3 < p_2 < p_1 < z; A_1(p_1) \wedge A_3(p_1,p_2,p_3) \}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle++%5Ccup+%5C%7Bp_1+p_2+p_3+p_4%3A+p_4+%3C+p_3+%3C+p_2+%3C+p_1+%3C+z%3B+A_1%28p_1%29+%5Cwedge+A_3%28p_1%2Cp_2%2Cp_3%29+%5C%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)
> 
> 
> ![Image 412: \displaystyle  \cup \dots](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle++%5Ccup+%5Cdots&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)
> 
> 
> and
> 
> 
> ![Image 413: \displaystyle  {\mathcal D}_- := \{ p_1 < z \} \cup \{ p_1 p_2: p_2 < p_1 < z; A_2(p_1,p_2)\}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle++%7B%5Cmathcal+D%7D_-+%3A%3D+%5C%7B+p_1+%3C+z+%5C%7D+%5Ccup+%5C%7B+p_1+p_2%3A+p_2+%3C+p_1+%3C+z%3B+A_2%28p_1%2Cp_2%29%5C%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)
> 
> 
> ![Image 414: \displaystyle  \cup \{p_1 p_2 p_3: p_3 < p_2 < p_1 < z; A_2(p_1,p_2) \}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle++%5Ccup+%5C%7Bp_1+p_2+p_3%3A+p_3+%3C+p_2+%3C+p_1+%3C+z%3B+A_2%28p_1%2Cp_2%29+%5C%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)
> 
> 
> ![Image 415: \displaystyle  \cup \{p_1 p_2 p_3 p_4: p_4 < p_3 < p_2 < p_1 < z; A_2(p_1,p_2) \wedge A_4(p_1,p_2,p_3,p_4) \}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle++%5Ccup+%5C%7Bp_1+p_2+p_3+p_4%3A+p_4+%3C+p_3+%3C+p_2+%3C+p_1+%3C+z%3B+A_2%28p_1%2Cp_2%29+%5Cwedge+A_4%28p_1%2Cp_2%2Cp_3%2Cp_4%29+%5C%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)
> 
> 
> ![Image 416: \displaystyle  \cup \dots.](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle++%5Ccup+%5Cdots.&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)
> 
> 
> *   (i) For any sets ![Image 417: {E_d \subset {\bf Z}}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BE_d+%5Csubset+%7B%5Cbf+Z%7D%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002), ![Image 418: {\sum_{d \in {\mathcal D}_+} \mu(d) 1_{E_d}}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Csum_%7Bd+%5Cin+%7B%5Cmathcal+D%7D_%2B%7D+%5Cmu%28d%29+1_%7BE_d%7D%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) is an upper bound sieve, and ![Image 419: {\sum_{d \in {\mathcal D}_-} \mu(d) 1_{E_d}}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Csum_%7Bd+%5Cin+%7B%5Cmathcal+D%7D_-%7D+%5Cmu%28d%29+1_%7BE_d%7D%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) is a lower bound sieve. In fact, we have the more precise identities 
> ![Image 420: \displaystyle  \sum_{d \in {\mathcal D}_+} \mu(d) 1_{E_d}(n) = 1_{n \not \in \bigcup_{p<z} E_p} + \sum_{r \hbox{ odd}} \sum_{d \in {\mathcal E}_r} 1_{n \in E_d} 1_{n \not \in \bigcup_{p<p_*(d)} E_p} ](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle++%5Csum_%7Bd+%5Cin+%7B%5Cmathcal+D%7D_%2B%7D+%5Cmu%28d%29+1_%7BE_d%7D%28n%29+%3D+1_%7Bn+%5Cnot+%5Cin+%5Cbigcup_%7Bp%3Cz%7D+E_p%7D+%2B+%5Csum_%7Br+%5Chbox%7B+odd%7D%7D+%5Csum_%7Bd+%5Cin+%7B%5Cmathcal+E%7D_r%7D+1_%7Bn+%5Cin+E_d%7D+1_%7Bn+%5Cnot+%5Cin+%5Cbigcup_%7Bp%3Cp_%2A%28d%29%7D+E_p%7D+&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)
> 
> 
> and
> 
> 
> ![Image 421: \displaystyle  \sum_{d \in {\mathcal D}_-} \mu(d) 1_{E_d}(n) = 1_{n \not \in \bigcup_{p<z} E_p} - \sum_{r \hbox{ even}} \sum_{d \in {\mathcal E}_r} 1_{n \in E_d} 1_{n \not \in \bigcup_{p<p_*(d)} E_p} ](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle++%5Csum_%7Bd+%5Cin+%7B%5Cmathcal+D%7D_-%7D+%5Cmu%28d%29+1_%7BE_d%7D%28n%29+%3D+1_%7Bn+%5Cnot+%5Cin+%5Cbigcup_%7Bp%3Cz%7D+E_p%7D+-+%5Csum_%7Br+%5Chbox%7B+even%7D%7D+%5Csum_%7Bd+%5Cin+%7B%5Cmathcal+E%7D_r%7D+1_%7Bn+%5Cin+E_d%7D+1_%7Bn+%5Cnot+%5Cin+%5Cbigcup_%7Bp%3Cp_%2A%28d%29%7D+E_p%7D+&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)
> 
> 
> where ![Image 422: {p_*(d)}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bp_%2A%28d%29%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) denotes the least prime factor of ![Image 423: {d}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bd%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) and, for each natural number ![Image 424: {r}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Br%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002), ![Image 425: {{\mathcal E}_r}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%7B%5Cmathcal+E%7D_r%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) is the collection of products ![Image 426: {p_1 \dots p_r}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bp_1+%5Cdots+p_r%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) with ![Image 427: {z > p_1 > \dots > p_r}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bz+%3E+p_1+%3E+%5Cdots+%3E+p_r%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) such that ![Image 428: {A_{r'}(p_1,\dots,p_{r'})}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BA_%7Br%27%7D%28p_1%2C%5Cdots%2Cp_%7Br%27%7D%29%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) holds for all ![Image 429: {r' < r}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Br%27+%3C+r%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) with the same parity as ![Image 430: {r}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Br%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002), but fails for ![Image 431: {r'=r}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Br%27%3Dr%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002). Thus for instance
> 
> 
> ![Image 432: \displaystyle  {\mathcal E}_3 = \{ p_1 p_2 p_3: z > p_1 > p_2 > p_3; A_1(p_1) \wedge \neg A_3(p_1,p_2,p_3) \}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle++%7B%5Cmathcal+E%7D_3+%3D+%5C%7B+p_1+p_2+p_3%3A+z+%3E+p_1+%3E+p_2+%3E+p_3%3B+A_1%28p_1%29+%5Cwedge+%5Cneg+A_3%28p_1%2Cp_2%2Cp_3%29+%5C%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)
> 
> 
> and
> 
> 
> ![Image 433: \displaystyle  {\mathcal E}_4 = \{ p_1 p_2 p_3 p_4: z > p_1 > p_2 > p_3 > p_4; ](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle++%7B%5Cmathcal+E%7D_4+%3D+%5C%7B+p_1+p_2+p_3+p_4%3A+z+%3E+p_1+%3E+p_2+%3E+p_3+%3E+p_4%3B+&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)
> 
> 
> ![Image 434: \displaystyle  A_2(p_1,p_2) \wedge \neg A_4(p_1,p_2,p_3,p_4) \}.](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle++A_2%28p_1%2Cp_2%29+%5Cwedge+%5Cneg+A_4%28p_1%2Cp_2%2Cp_3%2Cp_4%29+%5C%7D.&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)
> 
> *   (ii) The sequences ![Image 435: {(\mu(d) 1_{{\mathcal D}_+}(d))_{d|P(z)}}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%28%5Cmu%28d%29+1_%7B%7B%5Cmathcal+D%7D_%2B%7D%28d%29%29_%7Bd%7CP%28z%29%7D%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) and ![Image 436: {(\mu(d) 1_{{\mathcal D}_-}(d))_{d|P(z)}}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%28%5Cmu%28d%29+1_%7B%7B%5Cmathcal+D%7D_-%7D%28d%29%29_%7Bd%7CP%28z%29%7D%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) are upper and lower sieve coefficient sequences respectively. 
> *   (iii) For any multiplicative function ![Image 437: {g}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bg%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002), one has the identities [![Image 438: \displaystyle \sum_{d \in {\mathcal D}_+} \mu(d) g(d) = V(z) + \sum_{r \hbox{ odd}} \sum_{d \in {\mathcal E}_r} g(d) V( p_*(d) ) \ \ \ \ \ (17)](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle++%5Csum_%7Bd+%5Cin+%7B%5Cmathcal+D%7D_%2B%7D+%5Cmu%28d%29+g%28d%29+%3D+V%28z%29+%2B+%5Csum_%7Br+%5Chbox%7B+odd%7D%7D+%5Csum_%7Bd+%5Cin+%7B%5Cmathcal+E%7D_r%7D+g%28d%29+V%28+p_%2A%28d%29+%29+%5C+%5C+%5C+%5C+%5C+%2817%29&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/)
> [](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/) and [](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/)
> 
> [![Image 439: \displaystyle \sum_{d \in {\mathcal D}_-} \mu(d) g(d) = V(z) - \sum_{r \hbox{ even}} \sum_{d \in {\mathcal E}_r} g(d) V( p_*(d) ) \ \ \ \ \ (18)](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle++%5Csum_%7Bd+%5Cin+%7B%5Cmathcal+D%7D_-%7D+%5Cmu%28d%29+g%28d%29+%3D+V%28z%29+-+%5Csum_%7Br+%5Chbox%7B+even%7D%7D+%5Csum_%7Bd+%5Cin+%7B%5Cmathcal+E%7D_r%7D+g%28d%29+V%28+p_%2A%28d%29+%29+%5C+%5C+%5C+%5C+%5C+%2818%29&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/)
> [](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/) where ![Image 440: {V(w) := \prod_{p<w} (1-g(p))}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BV%28w%29+%3A%3D+%5Cprod_%7Bp%3Cw%7D+%281-g%28p%29%29%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002).

Note that the Legendre sieve is the special case of the combinatorial sieve when all the predicates ![Image 441: {A_r(p_1,\dots,p_r)}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BA_r%28p_1%2C%5Cdots%2Cp_r%29%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) are true for all choices of inputs ![Image 442: {p_1,\dots,p_r}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bp_1%2C%5Cdots%2Cp_r%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) (i.e. one does not ever utilise the trivial lower bound sieve); more generally, the Brun pure sieve with parameter ![Image 443: {k}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bk%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)corresponds to the case when ![Image 444: {A_r(p_1,\dots,p_r)}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BA_r%28p_1%2C%5Cdots%2Cp_r%29%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) is always true for ![Image 445: {r \leq k}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Br+%5Cleq+k%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) and always false for ![Image 446: {r>k}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Br%3Ek%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002). The predicates ![Image 447: {A_r}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BA_r%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) for odd ![Image 448: {r}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Br%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) are only used for the upper bound sieve and not the lower bound sieve; conversely, the predicates ![Image 449: {A_r}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BA_r%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) for even ![Image 450: {r}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Br%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) are only used for the lower bound sieve and not the upper bound sieve.

> **Exercise 15** Prove Proposition [14](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/#gencom).

> **Exercise 16** Interpret the sieves in Exercise [8](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/#easy-sieve) as combinatorial sieves. What are the predicates ![Image 451: {A_1, A_2}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BA_1%2C+A_2%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) in this case?

We now use the above proposition to attack Problem [10](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/#ideal) for a given choice of parameters ![Image 452: {z}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bz%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) and ![Image 453: {D}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BD%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002); we will focus on the regime when [](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/)

[![Image 454: \displaystyle z = D^{1/s} \ \ \ \ \ (19)](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle++z+%3D+D%5E%7B1%2Fs%7D+%5C+%5C+%5C+%5C+%5C+%2819%29&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/)
[](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/) for some ![Image 455: {s,z \geq 1}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bs%2Cz+%5Cgeq+1%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002), thus we are sifting out primes up to a small power of the sieve level ![Image 456: {D}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BD%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002). To do this, one needs to select the predicates ![Image 457: {A_r(p_1,\dots,p_r)}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BA_r%28p_1%2C%5Cdots%2Cp_r%29%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) in such a fashion that the sets ![Image 458: {{\mathcal D}_\pm}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%7B%5Cmathcal+D%7D_%5Cpm%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) only consist of divisors ![Image 459: {d|P(z)}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bd%7CP%28z%29%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) that are less than or equal to ![Image 460: {D}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BD%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002). There are a number of ways to achieve this, but experience has shown that a good general-purpose choice in this regard is to define ![Image 461: {A_r(p_1,\dots,p_r)}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BA_r%28p_1%2C%5Cdots%2Cp_r%29%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) to be the predicate [](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/)

[![Image 462: \displaystyle p_1 \dots p_{r-1} p_r^{\beta+1} \leq D \ \ \ \ \ (20)](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle++p_1+%5Cdots+p_%7Br-1%7D+p_r%5E%7B%5Cbeta%2B1%7D+%5Cleq+D+%5C+%5C+%5C+%5C+%5C+%2820%29&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/)
[](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/) for some parameter [](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/)

[![Image 463: \displaystyle 1 \leq \beta \leq s \ \ \ \ \ (21)](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle++1+%5Cleq+%5Cbeta+%5Cleq+s+%5C+%5C+%5C+%5C+%5C+%2821%29&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/)
[](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/) to be chosen later. The combinatorial sieve with this choice of predicate is known as the _beta sieve_ or _Rosser-Iwaniec sieve_ at sieve level ![Image 464: {D}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BD%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002). Observe that if ![Image 465: {p_1 \dots p_r}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bp_1+%5Cdots+p_r%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) lies in ![Image 466: {{\mathcal D}_\pm}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%7B%5Cmathcal+D%7D_%5Cpm%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) for some ![Image 467: {r \geq 2}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Br+%5Cgeq+2%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) and ![Image 468: {z > p_1 > \dots > p_r}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bz+%3E+p_1+%3E+%5Cdots+%3E+p_r%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002), then at least one of ![Image 469: {p_1 \dots p_{r-1} p_r^{\beta+1} \leq D}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bp_1+%5Cdots+p_%7Br-1%7D+p_r%5E%7B%5Cbeta%2B1%7D+%5Cleq+D%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) or ![Image 470: {p_1 \dots p_{r-2} p_{r-1}^{\beta+1} \leq D}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bp_1+%5Cdots+p_%7Br-2%7D+p_%7Br-1%7D%5E%7B%5Cbeta%2B1%7D+%5Cleq+D%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) will hold, and hence ![Image 471: {p_1 \dots p_r \leq D}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bp_1+%5Cdots+p_r+%5Cleq+D%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) since ![Image 472: {\beta \geq 1}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Cbeta+%5Cgeq+1%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002). If ![Image 473: {r \leq 1}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Br+%5Cleq+1%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002), we have the same conclusion thanks to [(19)](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/#zd) since ![Image 474: {s \geq 1}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bs+%5Cgeq+1%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002). Thus under the hypotheses [(21)](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/#beta), [(19)](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/#zd), the beta sieves are indeed upper and lower bound sieves at level ![Image 475: {D}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BD%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002).

Now we estimate the quantities [(14)](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/#dplus), [(15)](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/#dminus) for the beta sieve coefficients ![Image 476: {\lambda^\pm_d := \mu(d) 1_{{\mathcal D}_\pm}(d)}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Clambda%5E%5Cpm_d+%3A%3D+%5Cmu%28d%29+1_%7B%7B%5Cmathcal+D%7D_%5Cpm%7D%28d%29%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) for some multiplicative function ![Image 477: {g}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bg%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) with ![Image 478: {0 \leq g(p) \leq 1}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B0+%5Cleq+g%28p%29+%5Cleq+1%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) for all ![Image 479: {p}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bp%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002). In order to actually compute asymptotics, we assume the Mertens-like axiom [](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/)

[![Image 480: \displaystyle V(w) \ll_\kappa \left(\frac{\log z}{\log w}\right)^\kappa V(z) \ \ \ \ \ (22)](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle++V%28w%29+%5Cll_%5Ckappa+%5Cleft%28%5Cfrac%7B%5Clog+z%7D%7B%5Clog+w%7D%5Cright%29%5E%5Ckappa+V%28z%29+%5C+%5C+%5C+%5C+%5C+%2822%29&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/)
[](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/) whenever ![Image 481: {2 \leq w \leq z}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B2+%5Cleq+w+%5Cleq+z%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002), where ![Image 482: {V(z) := \prod_{p < z} (1 - g(p))}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BV%28z%29+%3A%3D+%5Cprod_%7Bp+%3C+z%7D+%281+-+g%28p%29%29%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) and some fixed ![Image 483: {\kappa>0}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Ckappa%3E0%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002), which we refer to as the _sieve dimension_. From Mertens’ theorems, we see that these axioms will in particular be obeyed if

![Image 484: \displaystyle  g(p) \leq \frac{\kappa}{p} + O_\kappa(\frac{1}{p^2})](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle++g%28p%29+%5Cleq+%5Cfrac%7B%5Ckappa%7D%7Bp%7D+%2B+O_%5Ckappa%28%5Cfrac%7B1%7D%7Bp%5E2%7D%29&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)

and

![Image 485: \displaystyle  g(p) \leq 1-c](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle++g%28p%29+%5Cleq+1-c&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)

for all primes ![Image 486: {p}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bp%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) and some fixed ![Image 487: {c>0}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bc%3E0%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) depending only on ![Image 488: {\kappa}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Ckappa%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002).

Let ![Image 489: {g}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bg%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) be as above. From [(17)](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/#lam), [(18)](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/#lam-2), [(22)](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/#goop) we see that the quantities [(14)](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/#dplus), [(15)](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/#dminus) are both of the form [](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/)

[![Image 490: \displaystyle V(z) \left( 1 + O_\kappa \left( \sum_r \sum_{d \in {\mathcal E}_r} g(d) \left( \frac{\log z}{\log p_*(d)} \right)^\kappa \right)\right). \ \ \ \ \ (23)](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle++V%28z%29+%5Cleft%28+1+%2B+O_%5Ckappa+%5Cleft%28+%5Csum_r+%5Csum_%7Bd+%5Cin+%7B%5Cmathcal+E%7D_r%7D+g%28d%29+%5Cleft%28+%5Cfrac%7B%5Clog+z%7D%7B%5Clog+p_%2A%28d%29%7D+%5Cright%29%5E%5Ckappa+%5Cright%29%5Cright%29.+%5C+%5C+%5C+%5C+%5C+%2823%29&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/)
[](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/) We now estimate the error term; our estimates will be somewhat crude in order to simplify the calculations, but this will already suffice for many applications. First observe that if ![Image 491: {z > p_1 > \dots > p_r}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bz+%3E+p_1+%3E+%5Cdots+%3E+p_r%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) then

![Image 492: \displaystyle  p_1 \dots p_{r-1} p_r^{\beta+1} \leq z^{r+\beta} = D^{(r+\beta)/s}.](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle++p_1+%5Cdots+p_%7Br-1%7D+p_r%5E%7B%5Cbeta%2B1%7D+%5Cleq+z%5E%7Br%2B%5Cbeta%7D+%3D+D%5E%7B%28r%2B%5Cbeta%29%2Fs%7D.&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)

We conclude that the condition ![Image 493: {A_r(p_1,\dots,p_r)}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BA_r%28p_1%2C%5Cdots%2Cp_r%29%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) is automatically satisfied when ![Image 494: {r+\beta \leq s}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Br%2B%5Cbeta+%5Cleq+s%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002), and so the ![Image 495: {r}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Br%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) summand in [(23)](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/#voz) can be restricted to the range ![Image 496: {r > s - \beta}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Br+%3E+s+-+%5Cbeta%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002).

Next, note that if ![Image 497: {d = p_1 \dots p_r \in {\mathcal E}_r}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bd+%3D+p_1+%5Cdots+p_r+%5Cin+%7B%5Cmathcal+E%7D_r%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) for some ![Image 498: {z > p_1 > \dots > p_r}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bz+%3E+p_1+%3E+%5Cdots+%3E+p_r%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002), then from [(20)](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/#arp) we have

![Image 499: \displaystyle  p_1 \dots p_{r'-1} p_{r'}^{\beta+1} \leq D](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle++p_1+%5Cdots+p_%7Br%27-1%7D+p_%7Br%27%7D%5E%7B%5Cbeta%2B1%7D+%5Cleq+D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)

for all ![Image 500: {1 \leq r' < r}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B1+%5Cleq+r%27+%3C+r%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) with the same parity as ![Image 501: {r}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Br%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002), which implies (somewhat crudely) that

![Image 502: \displaystyle  p_1 \dots p_{r'-1} p_{r'}^{\beta} \leq D](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle++p_1+%5Cdots+p_%7Br%27-1%7D+p_%7Br%27%7D%5E%7B%5Cbeta%7D+%5Cleq+D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)

for all ![Image 503: {1 \leq r' < r}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B1+%5Cleq+r%27+%3C+r%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002), regardless of parity (note that the ![Image 504: {r'=1}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Br%27%3D1%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) case follows since ![Image 505: {p_1^{\beta} \leq z^{\beta} = D^{\beta / s} \leq D}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bp_1%5E%7B%5Cbeta%7D+%5Cleq+z%5E%7B%5Cbeta%7D+%3D+D%5E%7B%5Cbeta+%2F+s%7D+%5Cleq+D%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)). We rearrange this inequality as

![Image 506: \displaystyle  \frac{D}{p_1 \dots p_{r'}} \geq \left( \frac{D}{p_1 \dots p_{r'-1}}\right)^{\frac{\beta-1}{\beta}}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle++%5Cfrac%7BD%7D%7Bp_1+%5Cdots+p_%7Br%27%7D%7D+%5Cgeq+%5Cleft%28+%5Cfrac%7BD%7D%7Bp_1+%5Cdots+p_%7Br%27-1%7D%7D%5Cright%29%5E%7B%5Cfrac%7B%5Cbeta-1%7D%7B%5Cbeta%7D%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)

which iterates to give

![Image 507: \displaystyle  \frac{D}{p_1 \dots p_{r-1}} \geq D^{(\frac{\beta-1}{\beta})^{r-1}}.](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle++%5Cfrac%7BD%7D%7Bp_1+%5Cdots+p_%7Br-1%7D%7D+%5Cgeq+D%5E%7B%28%5Cfrac%7B%5Cbeta-1%7D%7B%5Cbeta%7D%29%5E%7Br-1%7D%7D.&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)

On the other hand, from the definition of ![Image 508: {{\mathcal E}_r}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%7B%5Cmathcal+E%7D_r%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) we have

![Image 509: \displaystyle  p_1 \dots p_{r-1} p_r^{\beta+1} > D](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle++p_1+%5Cdots+p_%7Br-1%7D+p_r%5E%7B%5Cbeta%2B1%7D+%3E+D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)

which leads to a lower bound on ![Image 510: {p_r = p_*(d)}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bp_r+%3D+p_%2A%28d%29%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002):

![Image 511: \displaystyle  p_r > D^{(\frac{\beta-1}{\beta})^{r-1} / (\beta+1)} ](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle++p_r+%3E+D%5E%7B%28%5Cfrac%7B%5Cbeta-1%7D%7B%5Cbeta%7D%29%5E%7Br-1%7D+%2F+%28%5Cbeta%2B1%29%7D+&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)

![Image 512: \displaystyle  > D^{(\frac{\beta-1}{\beta})^{r} / \beta}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle++%3E+D%5E%7B%28%5Cfrac%7B%5Cbeta-1%7D%7B%5Cbeta%7D%29%5E%7Br%7D+%2F+%5Cbeta%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)

![Image 513: \displaystyle  > z^{(\frac{\beta-1}{\beta})^{r}}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle++%3E+z%5E%7B%28%5Cfrac%7B%5Cbeta-1%7D%7B%5Cbeta%7D%29%5E%7Br%7D%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)

so in particular

![Image 514: \displaystyle  \frac{\log z}{\log p_*(d)} < \left(\frac{\beta}{\beta-1}\right)^r.](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle++%5Cfrac%7B%5Clog+z%7D%7B%5Clog+p_%2A%28d%29%7D+%3C+%5Cleft%28%5Cfrac%7B%5Cbeta%7D%7B%5Cbeta-1%7D%5Cright%29%5Er.&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)

We can thus estimate [(23)](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/#voz) somewhat crudely as

![Image 515: \displaystyle  V(z) \left( 1 + O_\kappa \left( \sum_{r \geq s-\beta} \sum_{z > p_1 > \dots > p_r > z^{(\frac{\beta-1}{\beta})^{r\kappa}}} g(p_1) \dots g(p_r) \left(\frac{\beta}{\beta-1}\right)^r \right)\right).](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle++V%28z%29+%5Cleft%28+1+%2B+O_%5Ckappa+%5Cleft%28+%5Csum_%7Br+%5Cgeq+s-%5Cbeta%7D+%5Csum_%7Bz+%3E+p_1+%3E+%5Cdots+%3E+p_r+%3E+z%5E%7B%28%5Cfrac%7B%5Cbeta-1%7D%7B%5Cbeta%7D%29%5E%7Br%5Ckappa%7D%7D%7D+g%28p_1%29+%5Cdots+g%28p_r%29+%5Cleft%28%5Cfrac%7B%5Cbeta%7D%7B%5Cbeta-1%7D%5Cright%29%5Er+%5Cright%29%5Cright%29.&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)

We can estimate

![Image 516: \displaystyle  \sum_{z > p_1 > \dots > p_r > z^{(\frac{\beta-1}{\beta})^{r}}} g(p_1) \dots g(p_r) \leq \frac{1}{r!} (\sum_{z > p \geq z^{(\frac{\beta-1}{\beta})^{r}}} g(p))^r ](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle++%5Csum_%7Bz+%3E+p_1+%3E+%5Cdots+%3E+p_r+%3E+z%5E%7B%28%5Cfrac%7B%5Cbeta-1%7D%7B%5Cbeta%7D%29%5E%7Br%7D%7D%7D+g%28p_1%29+%5Cdots+g%28p_r%29+%5Cleq+%5Cfrac%7B1%7D%7Br%21%7D+%28%5Csum_%7Bz+%3E+p+%5Cgeq+z%5E%7B%28%5Cfrac%7B%5Cbeta-1%7D%7B%5Cbeta%7D%29%5E%7Br%7D%7D%7D+g%28p%29%29%5Er+&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)

![Image 517: \displaystyle  \leq \frac{1}{r!} (\log \prod_{z > p \geq z^{(\frac{\beta-1}{\beta})^{r}}} (1-g(p))^{-1})^r ](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle++%5Cleq+%5Cfrac%7B1%7D%7Br%21%7D+%28%5Clog+%5Cprod_%7Bz+%3E+p+%5Cgeq+z%5E%7B%28%5Cfrac%7B%5Cbeta-1%7D%7B%5Cbeta%7D%29%5E%7Br%7D%7D%7D+%281-g%28p%29%29%5E%7B-1%7D%29%5Er+&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)

![Image 518: \displaystyle  = \frac{1}{r!} ( \log( V(z^{(\frac{\beta-1}{\beta})^{r}}) / V(z) ) )^r ](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle++%3D+%5Cfrac%7B1%7D%7Br%21%7D+%28+%5Clog%28+V%28z%5E%7B%28%5Cfrac%7B%5Cbeta-1%7D%7B%5Cbeta%7D%29%5E%7Br%7D%7D%29+%2F+V%28z%29+%29+%29%5Er+&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)

![Image 519: \displaystyle  \ll \frac{1}{r!} ( \kappa r \log \frac{\beta}{\beta-1} + O(1) )^r ](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle++%5Cll+%5Cfrac%7B1%7D%7Br%21%7D+%28+%5Ckappa+r+%5Clog+%5Cfrac%7B%5Cbeta%7D%7B%5Cbeta-1%7D+%2B+O%281%29+%29%5Er+&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)

![Image 520: \displaystyle  \ll ( \kappa e \log \frac{\beta}{\beta-1} + O(1/r) )^r ](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle++%5Cll+%28+%5Ckappa+e+%5Clog+%5Cfrac%7B%5Cbeta%7D%7B%5Cbeta-1%7D+%2B+O%281%2Fr%29+%29%5Er+&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)

thanks to [(22)](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/#goop) and the crude bound ![Image 521: {\frac{r^r}{r!} \leq e^r}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Cfrac%7Br%5Er%7D%7Br%21%7D+%5Cleq+e%5Er%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) coming from the Taylor expansion of ![Image 522: {e^r}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Be%5Er%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002). If we choose ![Image 523: {\beta}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Cbeta%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) large enough so that [](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/)

[![Image 524: \displaystyle \kappa e (\log \frac{\beta}{\beta-1}) (\frac{\beta}{\beta-1})^\kappa \leq \frac{1}{e} \ \ \ \ \ (24)](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle++%5Ckappa+e+%28%5Clog+%5Cfrac%7B%5Cbeta%7D%7B%5Cbeta-1%7D%29+%28%5Cfrac%7B%5Cbeta%7D%7B%5Cbeta-1%7D%29%5E%5Ckappa+%5Cleq+%5Cfrac%7B1%7D%7Be%7D+%5C+%5C+%5C+%5C+%5C+%2824%29&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/)
[](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/) (note the left-hand side decays like ![Image 525: {\kappa/\beta}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Ckappa%2F%5Cbeta%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) as ![Image 526: {\beta \rightarrow \infty}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Cbeta+%5Crightarrow+%5Cinfty%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)), we thus can estimate [(23)](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/#voz) by

![Image 527: \displaystyle  V(z) \left( 1 + O_\kappa \left( \sum_{r \geq s-\beta} (\frac{1}{e} + O(1/r))^r \right)\right)](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle++V%28z%29+%5Cleft%28+1+%2B+O_%5Ckappa+%5Cleft%28+%5Csum_%7Br+%5Cgeq+s-%5Cbeta%7D+%28%5Cfrac%7B1%7D%7Be%7D+%2B+O%281%2Fr%29%29%5Er+%5Cright%29%5Cright%29&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)

which sums to

![Image 528: \displaystyle  V(z) ( 1 + O_{\kappa,\beta}( e^{-s} ) ).](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle++V%28z%29+%28+1+%2B+O_%7B%5Ckappa%2C%5Cbeta%7D%28+e%5E%7B-s%7D+%29+%29.&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)

We conclude

> **Lemma 17 (Fundamental lemma of sieve theory)** Let ![Image 529: {\kappa > 0}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Ckappa+%3E+0%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002). If ![Image 530: {z = D^{1/s}}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bz+%3D+D%5E%7B1%2Fs%7D%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) for some ![Image 531: {D,s \geq 1}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BD%2Cs+%5Cgeq+1%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002), then there exist combinatorial upper and lower sieve coefficients ![Image 532: {(\lambda^\pm_d)_{d \in {\mathcal D}}}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%28%5Clambda%5E%5Cpm_d%29_%7Bd+%5Cin+%7B%5Cmathcal+D%7D%7D%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) supported in ![Image 533: {{\mathcal D} := \{ d | P(z): d \leq D \}}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%7B%5Cmathcal+D%7D+%3A%3D+%5C%7B+d+%7C+P%28z%29%3A+d+%5Cleq+D+%5C%7D%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) such that [](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/)
> 
> [![Image 534: \displaystyle \sum_{d \in {\mathcal D}} \lambda^\pm_d g(d) = V(z) ( 1 + O_{\kappa}( e^{-s} ) ) \ \ \ \ \ (25)](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle++%5Csum_%7Bd+%5Cin+%7B%5Cmathcal+D%7D%7D+%5Clambda%5E%5Cpm_d+g%28d%29+%3D+V%28z%29+%28+1+%2B+O_%7B%5Ckappa%7D%28+e%5E%7B-s%7D+%29+%29+%5C+%5C+%5C+%5C+%5C+%2825%29&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/)
> [](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/) for any multiplicative function ![Image 535: {g}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bg%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) with ![Image 536: {0 \leq g(p) < 1}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B0+%5Cleq+g%28p%29+%3C+1%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) for all primes ![Image 537: {p}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bp%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002), obeying the bounds [(22)](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/#goop).

Informally, the fundamental lemma shows that one can get exponentially close to the Legendre sieve limit of ![Image 538: {V(z)}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BV%28z%29%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) as long as the sieve level ![Image 539: {D}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BD%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) is a large power of the sifting range ![Image 540: {z}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bz%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002).

_Proof:_ Without loss of generality we may assume that ![Image 541: {s}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bs%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) is sufficiently large depending on ![Image 542: {\kappa}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Ckappa%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002), since for small ![Image 543: {s}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bs%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) we may simply reduce ![Image 544: {z}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bz%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) (for the purposes of the upper bound sieve) or use the trivial lower bound sieve ![Image 545: {0}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B0%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002). But then we may find ![Image 546: {\beta = \beta(\kappa)}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Cbeta+%3D+%5Cbeta%28%5Ckappa%29%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) obeying [(24)](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/#beta-bong) and less than ![Image 547: {s}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bs%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002), and the claim follows. ![Image 548: \Box](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5CBox&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)

> **Exercise 18** Improve the ![Image 549: {O_\kappa( e^{-s} )}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BO_%5Ckappa%28+e%5E%7B-s%7D+%29%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) error in [(25)](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/#soap) to ![Image 550: {O_{\kappa,\varepsilon}( e^{-(1-\varepsilon) s \log s} )}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BO_%7B%5Ckappa%2C%5Cvarepsilon%7D%28+e%5E%7B-%281-%5Cvarepsilon%29+s+%5Clog+s%7D+%29%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) for any ![Image 551: {\varepsilon>0}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Cvarepsilon%3E0%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002). (It is possible to show that this error term is essentially best possible, at least in the linear dimension case ![Image 552: {\kappa = 1}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Ckappa+%3D+1%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002), by using asymptotics for rough numbers (Exercise 28 of [Supplement 4](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/04/254a-supplement-4-probabilistic-models-and-heuristics-for-the-primes-optional/)), but we will not do so here.)

The fundamental lemma is often used as a preliminary sifting device to remove small primes, in preparation for a more sophisticated sieve to deal with medium primes. But one can of course use it directly. Indeed, thanks to Theorem [5](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/#dual-sieve), we now have an adequate answer to Problem [3](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/#sivp) in the regime where the sieve dimension ![Image 553: {\kappa}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Ckappa%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) is fixed and the sifting level is small compared to the level of distribution:

> **Corollary 19 (Fundamental lemma, applied directly)**[](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/) Let ![Image 554: {z = D^{1/s}}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bz+%3D+D%5E%7B1%2Fs%7D%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) for some ![Image 555: {\kappa, s, D \geq 1}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Ckappa%2C+s%2C+D+%5Cgeq+1%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002), and suppose that ![Image 556: {g}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bg%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) is a multiplicative function obeying the bounds [(22)](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/#goop) for all ![Image 557: {2 \leq w \leq z}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B2+%5Cleq+w+%5Cleq+z%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002), where ![Image 558: {V(w) := \prod_{p<w} (1-g(p))}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BV%28w%29+%3A%3D+%5Cprod_%7Bp%3Cw%7D+%281-g%28p%29%29%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002). For each ![Image 559: {p \leq z}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bp+%5Cleq+z%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002), let ![Image 560: {E_p}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BE_p%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) be a set of integers, and let ![Image 561: {(a_n)_{n \in {\bf Z}}}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%28a_n%29_%7Bn+%5Cin+%7B%5Cbf+Z%7D%7D%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) is a finitely supported sequence of non-negative reals such that
> 
> 
> ![Image 562: \displaystyle  \sum_{n \in E_d} a_n = X g(d) + r_d](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle++%5Csum_%7Bn+%5Cin+E_d%7D+a_n+%3D+X+g%28d%29+%2B+r_d&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)
> 
> 
> for all square-free ![Image 563: {d \leq D}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bd+%5Cleq+D%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002), where ![Image 564: {X > 0}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BX+%3E+0%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) and ![Image 565: {r_d \in {\bf R}}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Br_d+%5Cin+%7B%5Cbf+R%7D%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002), and ![Image 566: {E_d := \bigcap_{p|d} E_p}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BE_d+%3A%3D+%5Cbigcap_%7Bp%7Cd%7D+E_p%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002). Then
> 
> 
> ![Image 567: \displaystyle  \sum_{n \not \in \bigcup_{p \leq z} E_p} a_n = (1 + O_\kappa(e^{-s})) X V(z) + O( \sum_{d\leq D: \mu^2(d)=1} |r_d| ).](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle++%5Csum_%7Bn+%5Cnot+%5Cin+%5Cbigcup_%7Bp+%5Cleq+z%7D+E_p%7D+a_n+%3D+%281+%2B+O_%5Ckappa%28e%5E%7B-s%7D%29%29+X+V%28z%29+%2B+O%28+%5Csum_%7Bd%5Cleq+D%3A+%5Cmu%5E2%28d%29%3D1%7D+%7Cr_d%7C+%29.&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)

We now specialise to the twin prime problem in Problem [2](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/#twin). Here, ![Image 568: {X = x}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BX+%3D+x%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002), and ![Image 569: {g(p)=\omega(p)/p}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bg%28p%29%3D%5Comega%28p%29%2Fp%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) where ![Image 570: {\omega(p)=2}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Comega%28p%29%3D2%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) for odd ![Image 571: {p}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bp%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002), with ![Image 572: {\omega(2)=1}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Comega%282%29%3D1%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002). In particular we may take ![Image 573: {\kappa = 2}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Ckappa+%3D+2%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002). We set ![Image 574: {z := x^{1/u}}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bz+%3A%3D+x%5E%7B1%2Fu%7D%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) for some fixed ![Image 575: {u > 1}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bu+%3E+1%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002), and ![Image 576: {D = x^{1-o(1)}}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BD+%3D+x%5E%7B1-o%281%29%7D%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) for some quantity ![Image 577: {o(1)}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bo%281%29%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) going sufficiently slowly to infinity, so ![Image 578: {s := u + o(1)}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bs+%3A%3D+u+%2B+o%281%29%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002). From [(8)](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/#pas) we have

![Image 579: \displaystyle  V(z) = (1+o(1)) \frac{u^2}{e^{2\gamma}} \frac{2\Pi_2}{\log^2 x} ](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle++V%28z%29+%3D+%281%2Bo%281%29%29+%5Cfrac%7Bu%5E2%7D%7Be%5E%7B2%5Cgamma%7D%7D+%5Cfrac%7B2%5CPi_2%7D%7B%5Clog%5E2+x%7D+&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)

and from [(5)](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/#sam) and the divisor bound we have

![Image 580: \displaystyle  r_d \leq \tau(d) \ll x^{o(1)}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle++r_d+%5Cleq+%5Ctau%28d%29+%5Cll+x%5E%7Bo%281%29%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)

for any ![Image 581: {d \leq D}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bd+%5Cleq+D%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002), as ![Image 582: {x \rightarrow \infty}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bx+%5Crightarrow+%5Cinfty%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002). From the fundamental lemma, we conclude that

![Image 583: \displaystyle  \pi_2(x, x^{1/u}) = \frac{u^2}{e^{2\gamma}} (1 + O( e^{-u} ) + o(1)) \frac{2\Pi_2 x}{\log^2 x} + O( x^{o(1)} D ).](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle++%5Cpi_2%28x%2C+x%5E%7B1%2Fu%7D%29+%3D+%5Cfrac%7Bu%5E2%7D%7Be%5E%7B2%5Cgamma%7D%7D+%281+%2B+O%28+e%5E%7B-u%7D+%29+%2B+o%281%29%29+%5Cfrac%7B2%5CPi_2+x%7D%7B%5Clog%5E2+x%7D+%2B+O%28+x%5E%7Bo%281%29%7D+D+%29.&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)

If we let the ![Image 584: {o(1)}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bo%281%29%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) term in ![Image 585: {D = x^{1-o(1)}}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BD+%3D+x%5E%7B1-o%281%29%7D%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) decay sufficiently slowly, then the final error term in the above expression can be absorbed into the second error term, and so [](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/)

[![Image 586: \displaystyle \pi_2(x, x^{1/u}) = \frac{u^2}{e^{2\gamma}} (1 + O( e^{-u} ) + o(1)) \frac{2\Pi_2 x}{\log^2 x}. \ \ \ \ \ (26)](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle++%5Cpi_2%28x%2C+x%5E%7B1%2Fu%7D%29+%3D+%5Cfrac%7Bu%5E2%7D%7Be%5E%7B2%5Cgamma%7D%7D+%281+%2B+O%28+e%5E%7B-u%7D+%29+%2B+o%281%29%29+%5Cfrac%7B2%5CPi_2+x%7D%7B%5Clog%5E2+x%7D.+%5C+%5C+%5C+%5C+%5C+%2826%29&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/)
[](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/) Setting ![Image 587: {u=2}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bu%3D2%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002), we conclude in particular that

![Image 588: \displaystyle  \pi_2(x, x^{1/2}) \ll \frac{x}{\log^2 x}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle++%5Cpi_2%28x%2C+x%5E%7B1%2F2%7D%29+%5Cll+%5Cfrac%7Bx%7D%7B%5Clog%5E2+x%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)

which implies from the sieve of Eratosthenes that there are ![Image 589: {O( \frac{x}{\log^2 x} )}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BO%28+%5Cfrac%7Bx%7D%7B%5Clog%5E2+x%7D+%29%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) pairs of twin primes in ![Image 590: {[x/2,x]}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Bx%2F2%2Cx%5D%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002). This implies a famous [theorem of Brun](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brun%27s_theorem):

> **Exercise 20 (Brun’s theorem)** Show that the sum ![Image 591: {\sum_{p: p+2 \in {\mathcal P}} \frac{1}{p}}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Csum_%7Bp%3A+p%2B2+%5Cin+%7B%5Cmathcal+P%7D%7D+%5Cfrac%7B1%7D%7Bp%7D%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) is convergent.

Alternatively, if one applies [(26)](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/#xu) with ![Image 592: {u}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bu%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) a sufficiently large natural number, we see that

![Image 593: \displaystyle  \pi_2(x,x^{1/u}) \gg \frac{x}{\log^2 x}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle++%5Cpi_2%28x%2Cx%5E%7B1%2Fu%7D%29+%5Cgg+%5Cfrac%7Bx%7D%7B%5Clog%5E2+x%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)

for all sufficiently large ![Image 594: {x}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bx%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002). This implies that there are ![Image 595: {\gg \frac{x}{\log^2 x}}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Cgg+%5Cfrac%7Bx%7D%7B%5Clog%5E2+x%7D%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) numbers ![Image 596: {n}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bn%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) in ![Image 597: {[1,x]}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5B1%2Cx%5D%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) with the property that ![Image 598: {n, n+2}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bn%2C+n%2B2%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) are both not divisible by any prime less than or equal to ![Image 599: {x^{1/u}}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bx%5E%7B1%2Fu%7D%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002). In particular (if ![Image 600: {n \leq x-2}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bn+%5Cleq+x-2%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)), ![Image 601: {n}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bn%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) and ![Image 602: {n+2}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bn%2B2%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) both have at most ![Image 603: {u}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bu%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) prime factors. We conclude a version of the twin prime conjecture for almost primes:

> **Corollary 21**[](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/) There is an absolute constant ![Image 604: {u}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bu%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) such that there are infinitely many natural numbers ![Image 605: {n}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bn%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) such that ![Image 606: {n, n+2}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bn%2C+n%2B2%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) are each the product of at most ![Image 607: {u}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bu%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) primes.

> **Exercise 22** By going through the beta sieve estimates carefully, show that one can take ![Image 608: {u=20}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bu%3D20%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) in the above corollary. (One can do substantially better if one uses more sophisticated sieve estimates, and of course [Chen’s theorem](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chen%27s_theorem) tells us that ![Image 609: {u}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bu%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) can be taken as low as ![Image 610: {2}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B2%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002); we will review the proof of Chen’s theorem in subsequent notes.)

In the above corollary, both elements of the pair ![Image 611: {n,n+2}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bn%2Cn%2B2%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) are permitted to be almost prime rather than prime. It is possible to use the fundamental lemma to improve upon this by forcing ![Image 612: {n}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bn%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) (for instance) to be prime. To do this, we run the twin prime sieve differently; rather than start with all integers and sieve out two residue classes modulo each prime ![Image 613: {p}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bp%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002), we instead start with the primes and sieve out one residue class modulo ![Image 614: {p}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bp%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002). More precisely, we consider the quantity

![Image 615: \displaystyle  \sum_{n \not \in \bigcup_{p \leq z} E_p} f(n)](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle++%5Csum_%7Bn+%5Cnot+%5Cin+%5Cbigcup_%7Bp+%5Cleq+z%7D+E_p%7D+f%28n%29&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)

where ![Image 616: {E_p}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BE_p%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) is the residue class ![Image 617: {-2\ (p)}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B-2%5C+%28p%29%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) and

![Image 618: \displaystyle  f(n) := \Lambda(n) 1_{[x/2,x-2]}(n) ](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle++f%28n%29+%3A%3D+%5CLambda%28n%29+1_%7B%5Bx%2F2%2Cx-2%5D%7D%28n%29+&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)

(say). Observe that the sum ![Image 619: {\sum_{n \in E_d} f(n)}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Csum_%7Bn+%5Cin+E_d%7D+f%28n%29%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) is only as large as ![Image 620: {O( \log^{O(1)} x)}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BO%28+%5Clog%5E%7BO%281%29%7D+x%29%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) at worst for even ![Image 621: {d}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bd%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002). For odd ![Image 622: {d}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bd%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002), we expect from the prime number theorem in arithmetic progressions that

![Image 623: \displaystyle  \sum_{n \in E_d} f(n) = \frac{1}{\phi(d)} \frac{x}{2} + r_d](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle++%5Csum_%7Bn+%5Cin+E_d%7D+f%28n%29+%3D+%5Cfrac%7B1%7D%7B%5Cphi%28d%29%7D+%5Cfrac%7Bx%7D%7B2%7D+%2B+r_d&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)

for some small ![Image 624: {r_d}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Br_d%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002). Thus it is natural to apply Corollary [19](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/#clob) with ![Image 625: {X := \frac{x}{2}}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BX+%3A%3D+%5Cfrac%7Bx%7D%7B2%7D%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) and ![Image 626: {g(d) := 1_{(d,2)=1} \frac{1}{\phi(d)}}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bg%28d%29+%3A%3D+1_%7B%28d%2C2%29%3D1%7D+%5Cfrac%7B1%7D%7B%5Cphi%28d%29%7D%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002), so that ![Image 627: {\kappa = 1}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Ckappa+%3D+1%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002). From the Bombieri-Vinogradov theorem (see Theorem 17 of [Notes 3](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/10/254a-notes-3-the-large-sieve-and-the-bombieri-vinogradov-theorem/)), we see that

![Image 628: \displaystyle  \sum_{d \leq x^{1/2-\varepsilon}} |r_d| \ll_{A,\varepsilon} x \log^{-A} x](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle++%5Csum_%7Bd+%5Cleq+x%5E%7B1%2F2-%5Cvarepsilon%7D%7D+%7Cr_d%7C+%5Cll_%7BA%2C%5Cvarepsilon%7D+x+%5Clog%5E%7B-A%7D+x&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)

for any fixed ![Image 629: {\varepsilon, A> 0}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Cvarepsilon%2C+A%3E+0%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002). We thus take ![Image 630: {D := x^{1/2-\varepsilon}}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BD+%3A%3D+x%5E%7B1%2F2-%5Cvarepsilon%7D%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) for some small fixed ![Image 631: {\varepsilon>0}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Cvarepsilon%3E0%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) and ![Image 632: {z := x^{1/u}}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bz+%3A%3D+x%5E%7B1%2Fu%7D%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002), and apply Corollary [19](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/#clob) to conclude that

![Image 633: \displaystyle  \sum_{n \not \in \bigcup_{p \leq z} E_p} f(n) = (1 + O( e^{-u/(1/2-\varepsilon)})) \frac{x}{2} \prod_{2 < p < z}( 1 - \frac{1}{\phi(p)})](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle++%5Csum_%7Bn+%5Cnot+%5Cin+%5Cbigcup_%7Bp+%5Cleq+z%7D+E_p%7D+f%28n%29+%3D+%281+%2B+O%28+e%5E%7B-u%2F%281%2F2-%5Cvarepsilon%29%7D%29%29+%5Cfrac%7Bx%7D%7B2%7D+%5Cprod_%7B2+%3C+p+%3C+z%7D%28+1+-+%5Cfrac%7B1%7D%7B%5Cphi%28p%29%7D%29&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)

![Image 634: \displaystyle  + O_{A,\varepsilon}( x \log^{-A} x ).](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle++%2B+O_%7BA%2C%5Cvarepsilon%7D%28+x+%5Clog%5E%7B-A%7D+x+%29.&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)

For ![Image 635: {u}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bu%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) a sufficiently large fixed constant, we conclude from Mertens’ theorem that

![Image 636: \displaystyle  \sum_{n \not \in \bigcup_{p \leq z} E_p} f(n) \gg \frac{x}{\log x} ](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle++%5Csum_%7Bn+%5Cnot+%5Cin+%5Cbigcup_%7Bp+%5Cleq+z%7D+E_p%7D+f%28n%29+%5Cgg+%5Cfrac%7Bx%7D%7B%5Clog+x%7D+&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)

for ![Image 637: {x}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bx%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) large enough. The contribution for those ![Image 638: {n}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bn%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) for which ![Image 639: {n}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bn%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) is a power of a prime, rather than a prime, is easily seen to be negligible, and we conclude that there are ![Image 640: {\gg \frac{x}{\log^2 x}}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Cgg+%5Cfrac%7Bx%7D%7B%5Clog%5E2+x%7D%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) primes ![Image 641: {p}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bp%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) less than ![Image 642: {x-2}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bx-2%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) such that ![Image 643: {p+2}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bp%2B2%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) has no factors less than ![Image 644: {x^{1/u}}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bx%5E%7B1%2Fu%7D%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002), and thus is the product of at most ![Image 645: {u}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bu%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) primes. We thus have the following improvement of Corollary [21](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/#sting):

> **Proposition 23** There is an absolute constant ![Image 646: {u}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bu%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) such that there are infinitely many primes ![Image 647: {p}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bp%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) such that ![Image 648: {p+2}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bp%2B2%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) is the product of at most ![Image 649: {u}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bu%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) primes.

More generally, the fundamental lemma (combined with equidistribution results such as the Bombieri-Vinogradov theorem) lets one prove analogues of various unsolved conjectures about primes, in which one either (a) is content with upper bounds of the right order of magnitude, or (b) is willing to replace primes with a suitable notion of almost prime. We give some examples in the exercises below.

> **Exercise 24 (Approximations to the prime ![Image 650: {k}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bk%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)-tuples conjecture)**[](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/) Let ![Image 651: {(h_1,\dots,h_k)}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%28h_1%2C%5Cdots%2Ch_k%29%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) be an admissible ![Image 652: {k}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bk%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)-tuple of integers for some ![Image 653: {k \geq 1}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bk+%5Cgeq+1%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002), thus the ![Image 654: {h_i}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bh_i%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) are all distinct, and for any ![Image 655: {p}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bp%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002), the number ![Image 656: {\omega(p)}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Comega%28p%29%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) of residue classes occupied by ![Image 657: {h_1,\dots,h_k\ (p)}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bh_1%2C%5Cdots%2Ch_k%5C+%28p%29%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) is never equal to ![Image 658: {p}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bp%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002). Let ![Image 659: {{\mathfrak S}}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%7B%5Cmathfrak+S%7D%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) denote the singular series [](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/)
> 
> [![Image 660: \displaystyle {\mathfrak S} := \prod_p (1-\frac{1}{p})^{-k} (1-\frac{\omega(p)}{p}). \ \ \ \ \ (27)](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle++%7B%5Cmathfrak+S%7D+%3A%3D+%5Cprod_p+%281-%5Cfrac%7B1%7D%7Bp%7D%29%5E%7B-k%7D+%281-%5Cfrac%7B%5Comega%28p%29%7D%7Bp%7D%29.+%5C+%5C+%5C+%5C+%5C+%2827%29&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/)

> **Exercise 25 (Approximations to the even Goldbach conjecture)** If ![Image 661: {N}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BN%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) is an even natural number, define the singular series
> 
> 
> ![Image 662: \displaystyle  {\mathfrak S}(N) := 2 \Pi_2 \prod_{p>2: p|N} \frac{p-1}{p-2}.](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle++%7B%5Cmathfrak+S%7D%28N%29+%3A%3D+2+%5CPi_2+%5Cprod_%7Bp%3E2%3A+p%7CN%7D+%5Cfrac%7Bp-1%7D%7Bp-2%7D.&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)

> **Exercise 26 (Approximations to Legendre’s conjecture)**

> **Exercise 27 (Approximations to Landau’s fourth problem)** Define the singular series
> 
> 
> ![Image 663: \displaystyle  {\mathfrak S} := \prod_p \frac{1-\omega(p)/p}{1-1/p}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle++%7B%5Cmathfrak+S%7D+%3A%3D+%5Cprod_p+%5Cfrac%7B1-%5Comega%28p%29%2Fp%7D%7B1-1%2Fp%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)
> 
> 
> where ![Image 664: {\omega(p)}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Comega%28p%29%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) is the number of residue classes ![Image 665: {a\ (p)}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Ba%5C+%28p%29%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) such that ![Image 666: {a^2 + 1 =0\ (p)}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Ba%5E2+%2B+1+%3D0%5C+%28p%29%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002).

**— 2. The Selberg upper bound sieve —**

We now turn to the _Selberg upper bound sieve_, which is a simple but remarkably useful general-purpose upper bound sieve. We again follow the [discussion of Friedlander and Iwaniec](http://www.ams.org/mathscinet-getitem?mr=2647984).

The idea of the sieve comes from the following trivial observation: if ![Image 667: {P}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BP%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) is a squarefree number, ![Image 668: {E_p}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BE_p%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) is a set of integers for each ![Image 669: {p|P}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bp%7CP%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002), and ![Image 670: {(\rho_d)_{d|P}}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%28%5Crho_d%29_%7Bd%7CP%7D%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) are arbitrary real numbers with ![Image 671: {\rho_1 = 1}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Crho_1+%3D+1%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002), then the function

![Image 672: \displaystyle  \left( \sum_{d|P} \rho_d 1_{E_d} \right)^2](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle++%5Cleft%28+%5Csum_%7Bd%7CP%7D+%5Crho_d+1_%7BE_d%7D+%5Cright%29%5E2&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)

is an upper bound sieve, since it is clearly non-negative and equals ![Image 673: {1}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B1%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) outside of ![Image 674: {\bigcup_{p|P} E_p}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Cbigcup_%7Bp%7CP%7D+E_p%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002). Equivalently, the sequence [](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/)

[![Image 675: \displaystyle \lambda^+_d := \sum_{d_1,d_2: [d_1,d_2] = d} \rho_{d_1} \rho_{d_2} \ \ \ \ \ (28)](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle++%5Clambda%5E%2B_d+%3A%3D+%5Csum_%7Bd_1%2Cd_2%3A+%5Bd_1%2Cd_2%5D+%3D+d%7D+%5Crho_%7Bd_1%7D+%5Crho_%7Bd_2%7D+%5C+%5C+%5C+%5C+%5C+%2828%29&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/)
[](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/) for ![Image 676: {d|P}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bd%7CP%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) is a sequence of upper bound sieve coefficients, where ![Image 677: {[d_1,d_2]}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Bd_1%2Cd_2%5D%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) is the least common multiple of ![Image 678: {d_1,d_2}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bd_1%2Cd_2%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002). If we set ![Image 679: {D=R^2}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BD%3DR%5E2%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) and assume that the ![Image 680: {\rho_d}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Crho_d%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) are only non-zero for ![Image 681: {d \leq R}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bd+%5Cleq+R%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002), then this sequence of sieve coefficients will only be non-zero on ![Image 682: {{\mathcal D} := \{ d|P: d \leq D \}}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%7B%5Cmathcal+D%7D+%3A%3D+%5C%7B+d%7CP%3A+d+%5Cleq+D+%5C%7D%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002). We will refer to sieves (and sieve coefficients) constructed by this method as _Selberg upper bound sieves_ (or _Selberg upper bound sieve coefficients_); they are also referred to as ![Image 683: {\rho^2}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Crho%5E2%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) sieves in the literature.

A key advantage of the Selberg sieve is that the coefficients ![Image 684: {\rho_d}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Crho_d%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) for ![Image 685: {1 < d \leq R}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B1+%3C+d+%5Cleq+R%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) are completely unconstrained within the real numbers, and the sieve depends in a quadratic fashion on these coefficients, so the problem of optimising the Selberg sieve for a given application usually reduces to an unconstrained quadratic optimisation problem, which can often be solved exactly in many cases. Of course, the optimal Selberg sieve need not be the globally optimal sieve amongst _all_ upper bound sieves; but in practice the Selberg sieve tends to give quite good results even if it might not be the globally optimal choice.

Selberg sieves can be used for many purposes, but we begin with the classical sieve problem posed in Problem [10](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/#ideal). To avoid some degeneracies we will assume that [](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/)

[![Image 686: \displaystyle 0 < g(p) < 1 \ \ \ \ \ (29)](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle++0+%3C+g%28p%29+%3C+1+%5C+%5C+%5C+%5C+%5C+%2829%29&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/)
[](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/) for all ![Image 687: {p|P}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bp%7CP%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002). Restricting to upper bound sieve coefficients of the Selberg form, the quantity [(14)](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/#dplus) becomes [](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/)

[![Image 688: \displaystyle \sum_{d_1,d_2|P} \rho_{d_1} \rho_{d_2} g( [d_1,d_2] ). \ \ \ \ \ (30)](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle++%5Csum_%7Bd_1%2Cd_2%7CP%7D+%5Crho_%7Bd_1%7D+%5Crho_%7Bd_2%7D+g%28+%5Bd_1%2Cd_2%5D+%29.+%5C+%5C+%5C+%5C+%5C+%2830%29&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/)
[](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/) This is a quadratic form in the coefficients ![Image 689: {\rho_d}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Crho_d%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002); our task is to minimise this amongst all choices of coefficients with ![Image 690: {\rho_1=1}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Crho_1%3D1%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002), with ![Image 691: {\rho_d}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Crho_d%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) vanishing for ![Image 692: {d>R}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bd%3ER%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002).

Observe that any ![Image 693: {d_1,d_2|P}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bd_1%2Cd_2%7CP%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) can be expressed as ![Image 694: {d_1 = a_1 b}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bd_1+%3D+a_1+b%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002), ![Image 695: {d_2 = a_2 b}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bd_2+%3D+a_2+b%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) with ![Image 696: {a_1 a_2 b | P}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Ba_1+a_2+b+%7C+P%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002), in which case ![Image 697: {[d_1,d_2] = a_1 a_2 b}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Bd_1%2Cd_2%5D+%3D+a_1+a_2+b%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002). By multiplicativity, we may thus write [(30)](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/#dor) as

![Image 698: \displaystyle  \sum_{a_1 a_2 b|P} \rho_{a_1 b} \rho_{a_2 b} g(b) g(a_1) g(a_2) ](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle++%5Csum_%7Ba_1+a_2+b%7CP%7D+%5Crho_%7Ba_1+b%7D+%5Crho_%7Ba_2+b%7D+g%28b%29+g%28a_1%29+g%28a_2%29+&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)

which we can rearrange as as

![Image 699: \displaystyle  \sum_{b|P} g(b) \sum_{a_1,a_2 | P/b: (a_1,a_2)=1} \rho_{a_1b} g(a_1) \rho_{a_2 b} g(a_2).](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle++%5Csum_%7Bb%7CP%7D+g%28b%29+%5Csum_%7Ba_1%2Ca_2+%7C+P%2Fb%3A+%28a_1%2Ca_2%29%3D1%7D+%5Crho_%7Ba_1b%7D+g%28a_1%29+%5Crho_%7Ba_2+b%7D+g%28a_2%29.&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)

The inner sum almost factorises as a square, but we have the coprimality constraint ![Image 700: {(a_1,a_2)=1}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%28a_1%2Ca_2%29%3D1%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) to deal with. The standard trick for dealing with this constraint is Möbius inversion:

![Image 701: \displaystyle  1_{(a_1,a_2)=1} = \sum_{d |(a_1,a_2)} \mu(d)](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle++1_%7B%28a_1%2Ca_2%29%3D1%7D+%3D+%5Csum_%7Bd+%7C%28a_1%2Ca_2%29%7D+%5Cmu%28d%29&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)
