of the above sieve, where ![Image 1032: {W = \prod_{p \leq w} p}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BW+%3D+%5Cprod_%7Bp+%5Cleq+w%7D+p%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) for some ![Image 1033: {w}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bw%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) slowly growing to infinity with ![Image 1034: {x}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bx%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) (e.g. ![Image 1035: {w = \log\log\log x}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bw+%3D+%5Clog%5Clog%5Clog+x%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) will do), and ![Image 1036: {b\ (W)}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bb%5C+%28W%29%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) is a residue class such that ![Image 1037: {b+h_i\ (W)}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bb%2Bh_i%5C+%28W%29%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) is primitive for all ![Image 1038: {i=1,\dots,k}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bi%3D1%2C%5Cdots%2Ck%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) (such a ![Image 1039: {b}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bb%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) exists thanks to the admissibility of ![Image 1040: {(h_1,\dots,h_k)}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%28h_1%2C%5Cdots%2Ch_k%29%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) and the Chinese remainder theorem). Note that for ![Image 1041: {w}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bw%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) large enough, the ![Image 1042: {n+h_i}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bn%2Bh_i%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) have no common factors, and so the ![Image 1043: {d_1,\dots,d_k}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bd_1%2C%5Cdots%2Cd_k%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) are automatically coprime to each other and to ![Image 1044: {W}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BW%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002). (In Maynard’s paper, the coefficients ![Image 1045: {f( \frac{\log d_1}{\log R}, \dots, \frac{\log d_k}{\log R} )}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bf%28+%5Cfrac%7B%5Clog+d_1%7D%7B%5Clog+R%7D%2C+%5Cdots%2C+%5Cfrac%7B%5Clog+d_k%7D%7B%5Clog+R%7D+%29%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) were replaced by a sequence ![Image 1046: {\rho_{d_1,\dots,d_k}}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Crho_%7Bd_1%2C%5Cdots%2Cd_k%7D%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) which was not directly arising from a smooth function ![Image 1047: {f}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bf%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002), but instead the analogue of the ![Image 1048: {y_{m_1,\dots,m_k}}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7By_%7Bm_1%2C%5Cdots%2Cm_k%7D%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) coefficients from the preceding section were chosen to be of the form ![Image 1049: {F( \frac{\log d_1}{\log R}, \dots, \frac{\log d_k}{\log R} )}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BF%28+%5Cfrac%7B%5Clog+d_1%7D%7B%5Clog+R%7D%2C+%5Cdots%2C+%5Cfrac%7B%5Clog+d_k%7D%7B%5Clog+R%7D+%29%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) for some suitable function ![Image 1050: {F}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BF%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002). However, the differences between the two approaches are fairly minor.)

It is possible to estimate the right-hand side of [(45)](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/#nund) using a modification of the arguments of the previous section:

> **Exercise 40**[](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/) Let ![Image 1051: {k, h_1,\dots,h_k, b, W, \theta, R, f, \nu}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bk%2C+h_1%2C%5Cdots%2Ch_k%2C+b%2C+W%2C+%5Ctheta%2C+R%2C+f%2C+%5Cnu%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) be as in the above discussion. Assume that ![Image 1052: {0 < \theta < 1}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B0+%3C+%5Ctheta+%3C+1%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002). If ![Image 1053: {w}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bw%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) goes to infinity at a sufficiently slow rate at ![Image 1054: {x \rightarrow \infty}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bx+%5Crightarrow+%5Cinfty%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002), establish the asymptotic
> 
> 
> ![Image 1055: \displaystyle  \sum_n \nu(n) = \left( \frac{W}{\phi(W)} \right)^k \frac{x}{2W\log^k R} (I(f) + o(1))](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle++%5Csum_n+%5Cnu%28n%29+%3D+%5Cleft%28+%5Cfrac%7BW%7D%7B%5Cphi%28W%29%7D+%5Cright%29%5Ek+%5Cfrac%7Bx%7D%7B2W%5Clog%5Ek+R%7D+%28I%28f%29+%2B+o%281%29%29&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)
> 
> 
> as ![Image 1056: {x \rightarrow \infty}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bx+%5Crightarrow+%5Cinfty%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002), where
> 
> 
> ![Image 1057: \displaystyle  I(f) := \int_{[0,+\infty)^k} f_{1,\dots,k}(t_1,\dots,t_k)^2\ dt_1 \dots dt_k](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle++I%28f%29+%3A%3D+%5Cint_%7B%5B0%2C%2B%5Cinfty%29%5Ek%7D+f_%7B1%2C%5Cdots%2Ck%7D%28t_1%2C%5Cdots%2Ct_k%29%5E2%5C+dt_1+%5Cdots+dt_k&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)
> 
> 
> and ![Image 1058: {f_{1,\dots,k}}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bf_%7B1%2C%5Cdots%2Ck%7D%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) denotes the mixed partial derivative ![Image 1059: {\frac{\partial^k f}{\partial t_1 \dots \partial t_k}}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Cfrac%7B%5Cpartial%5Ek+f%7D%7B%5Cpartial+t_1+%5Cdots+%5Cpartial+t_k%7D%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) in each of the ![Image 1060: {k}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bk%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) variables ![Image 1061: {t_1,\dots,t_k}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bt_1%2C%5Cdots%2Ct_k%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002).

Now we consider a term on the left-hand side, say ![Image 1062: {\sum_n \nu(n) 1_{n+h_k \hbox{ prime}}}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Csum_n+%5Cnu%28n%29+1_%7Bn%2Bh_k+%5Chbox%7B+prime%7D%7D%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002). When ![Image 1063: {n+h_k}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bn%2Bh_k%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) is prime, the quantity ![Image 1064: {d_k}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bd_k%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) in [(46)](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/#nunda) is necessarily ![Image 1065: {1}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B1%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002), so [(46)](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/#nunda) simplifies to a ![Image 1066: {k-1}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bk-1%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)-dimensional version of itself:

![Image 1067: \displaystyle  \nu(n) = 1_{n=b\ (W)} 1_{[x/2,x]}(n) \times](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle++%5Cnu%28n%29+%3D+1_%7Bn%3Db%5C+%28W%29%7D+1_%7B%5Bx%2F2%2Cx%5D%7D%28n%29+%5Ctimes&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)

![Image 1068: \displaystyle \left( \sum_{d_i | n+h_i \forall i=1,\dots,k-1} \mu(d_1) \dots \mu(d_{k-1}) f( \frac{\log d_1}{\log R}, \dots, \frac{\log d_{k-1}}{\log R}, 0 ) \right)^2.](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle+%5Cleft%28+%5Csum_%7Bd_i+%7C+n%2Bh_i+%5Cforall+i%3D1%2C%5Cdots%2Ck-1%7D+%5Cmu%28d_1%29+%5Cdots+%5Cmu%28d_%7Bk-1%7D%29+f%28+%5Cfrac%7B%5Clog+d_1%7D%7B%5Clog+R%7D%2C+%5Cdots%2C+%5Cfrac%7B%5Clog+d_%7Bk-1%7D%7D%7B%5Clog+R%7D%2C+0+%29+%5Cright%29%5E2.&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)

If ![Image 1069: {\theta < \frac{1}{2}}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Ctheta+%3C+%5Cfrac%7B1%7D%7B2%7D%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002), one can control the effect of the weight ![Image 1070: {1_{n+h_k \hbox{ prime}}}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B1_%7Bn%2Bh_k+%5Chbox%7B+prime%7D%7D%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) using the Bombieri-Vinogradov theorem, and after some moderately complicated calculations one obtains

> **Exercise 41**[](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/) Let ![Image 1071: {k, h_1,\dots,h_k, b, W, \theta, R, f, \nu}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bk%2C+h_1%2C%5Cdots%2Ch_k%2C+b%2C+W%2C+%5Ctheta%2C+R%2C+f%2C+%5Cnu%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) be as in the above discussion. Assume that ![Image 1072: {0 < \theta < 1/2}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B0+%3C+%5Ctheta+%3C+1%2F2%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002). If ![Image 1073: {w}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bw%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) goes to infinity at a sufficiently slow rate at ![Image 1074: {x \rightarrow \infty}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bx+%5Crightarrow+%5Cinfty%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002), establish the asymptotic
> 
> 
> ![Image 1075: \displaystyle  \sum_n \nu(n) 1_{n+h_k \in {\mathcal P}} = \left( \frac{W}{\phi(W)} \right)^k \frac{x}{2W\log^{k-1} R \log x} (J_k(f) + o(1))](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle++%5Csum_n+%5Cnu%28n%29+1_%7Bn%2Bh_k+%5Cin+%7B%5Cmathcal+P%7D%7D+%3D+%5Cleft%28+%5Cfrac%7BW%7D%7B%5Cphi%28W%29%7D+%5Cright%29%5Ek+%5Cfrac%7Bx%7D%7B2W%5Clog%5E%7Bk-1%7D+R+%5Clog+x%7D+%28J_k%28f%29+%2B+o%281%29%29&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)
> 
> 
> as ![Image 1076: {x \rightarrow \infty}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bx+%5Crightarrow+%5Cinfty%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002), where
> 
> 
> ![Image 1077: \displaystyle  J_k(f) := \int_{[0,+\infty)^{k-1}} f_{1,\dots,k-1}(t_1,\dots,t_{k-1},0)^2\ dt_1 \dots dt_{k-1}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle++J_k%28f%29+%3A%3D+%5Cint_%7B%5B0%2C%2B%5Cinfty%29%5E%7Bk-1%7D%7D+f_%7B1%2C%5Cdots%2Ck-1%7D%28t_1%2C%5Cdots%2Ct_%7Bk-1%7D%2C0%29%5E2%5C+dt_1+%5Cdots+dt_%7Bk-1%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)
> 
> 
> and ![Image 1078: {f_{1,\dots,k-1}}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bf_%7B1%2C%5Cdots%2Ck-1%7D%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) denotes the mixed partial derivative ![Image 1079: {\frac{\partial^{k-1} f}{\partial t_1 \dots \partial t_{k-1}}}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Cfrac%7B%5Cpartial%5E%7Bk-1%7D+f%7D%7B%5Cpartial+t_1+%5Cdots+%5Cpartial+t_%7Bk-1%7D%7D%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) in the ![Image 1080: {k-1}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bk-1%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) variables ![Image 1081: {t_1,\dots,t_{k-1}}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bt_1%2C%5Cdots%2Ct_%7Bk-1%7D%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002). Similarly for permutations of the ![Image 1082: {1,\dots,k}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B1%2C%5Cdots%2Ck%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) indices.

In view of these two calculations, one can establish Maynard’s theorem as soon as one can find ![Image 1083: {k}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bk%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) and ![Image 1084: {f}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bf%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) for which

![Image 1085: \displaystyle  \sum_{i=1}^k J_i(f) > \frac{m}{\theta/2} I(f)](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle++%5Csum_%7Bi%3D1%7D%5Ek+J_i%28f%29+%3E+%5Cfrac%7Bm%7D%7B%5Ctheta%2F2%7D+I%28f%29&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)

where ![Image 1086: {J_i}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BJ_i%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) is defined similarly to ![Image 1087: {J_k}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BJ_k%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) but with the role of the ![Image 1088: {i}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bi%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) and ![Image 1089: {k}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bk%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) indices swapped. This is now a problem purely in calculus of variations rather than number theory. It is thus natural to take ![Image 1090: {\theta}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Ctheta%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) close to ![Image 1091: {1/2}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B1%2F2%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) (though, for the qualitative analysis performed here, actually any non-zero choice of ![Image 1092: {\theta}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Ctheta%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) independent of ![Image 1093: {k}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bk%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) will suffice). Our task is now to find ![Image 1094: {k,f}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bk%2Cf%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) with

![Image 1095: \displaystyle  \sum_{i=1}^k J_i(f) > 4 m I(f).](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle++%5Csum_%7Bi%3D1%7D%5Ek+J_i%28f%29+%3E+4+m+I%28f%29.&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)

The optimal choice of ![Image 1096: {f}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bf%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) for a given ![Image 1097: {k}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bk%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) is not known for any ![Image 1098: {k>2}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bk%3E2%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002), but one can generate a “good enough” choice of ![Image 1099: {f}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bf%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) by the following set of calculations. Firstly, we restrict to the case when ![Image 1100: {f(t_1,\dots,t_k)}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bf%28t_1%2C%5Cdots%2Ct_k%29%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) is symmetric in the ![Image 1101: {t_1,\dots,t_k}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bt_1%2C%5Cdots%2Ct_k%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002), so that all the ![Image 1102: {J_i}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BJ_i%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) are equal and our objective is now to satisfy the inequality

![Image 1103: \displaystyle  kJ_k(f) > 4 m I(f).](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle++kJ_k%28f%29+%3E+4+m+I%28f%29.&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)

Next, we make the change of variables ![Image 1104: {F := f_{1,\dots,k}}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BF+%3A%3D+f_%7B1%2C%5Cdots%2Ck%7D%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002), thus ![Image 1105: {F}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BF%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) is smooth on ![Image 1106: {[0,+\infty)^k}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5B0%2C%2B%5Cinfty%29%5Ek%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) and supported on the simplex ![Image 1107: {\{t_1+\dots+t_k \leq 1\}}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5C%7Bt_1%2B%5Cdots%2Bt_k+%5Cleq+1%5C%7D%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) but is otherwise arbitrary on this simplex. From the fundamental theorem of calculus, the desired inequality now becomes

![Image 1108: \displaystyle  k \int_{[0,+\infty)^{k-1}} (\int_0^\infty F(t_1,\dots,t_k)\ dt_k)^2 dt_1 \dots dt_{k-1} ](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle++k+%5Cint_%7B%5B0%2C%2B%5Cinfty%29%5E%7Bk-1%7D%7D+%28%5Cint_0%5E%5Cinfty+F%28t_1%2C%5Cdots%2Ct_k%29%5C+dt_k%29%5E2+dt_1+%5Cdots+dt_%7Bk-1%7D+&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)

![Image 1109: \displaystyle > 4m \int_{[0,+\infty)^k} F(t_1,\dots,t_k)^2\ dt_1 \dots t_k.](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle+%3E+4m+%5Cint_%7B%5B0%2C%2B%5Cinfty%29%5Ek%7D+F%28t_1%2C%5Cdots%2Ct_k%29%5E2%5C+dt_1+%5Cdots+t_k.&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)

We select a function ![Image 1110: {F}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BF%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) of the truncated product form

![Image 1111: \displaystyle  F(t_1,\dots,t_k) = k^{1/2} g(k t_1) \dots k^{1/2} g(k t_k) 1_{t_1 + \dots +t_k < 1}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle++F%28t_1%2C%5Cdots%2Ct_k%29+%3D+k%5E%7B1%2F2%7D+g%28k+t_1%29+%5Cdots+k%5E%7B1%2F2%7D+g%28k+t_k%29+1_%7Bt_1+%2B+%5Cdots+%2Bt_k+%3C+1%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)

where ![Image 1112: {g: [0,+\infty) \rightarrow {\bf R}^+}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bg%3A+%5B0%2C%2B%5Cinfty%29+%5Crightarrow+%7B%5Cbf+R%7D%5E%2B%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) is a smooth non-negative function normalised so that [](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/)

[![Image 1113: \displaystyle \int_0^\infty g(t)^2\ dt = 1 \ \ \ \ \ (47)](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle++%5Cint_0%5E%5Cinfty+g%28t%29%5E2%5C+dt+%3D+1+%5C+%5C+%5C+%5C+%5C+%2847%29&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/)
[](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/) so that

![Image 1114: \displaystyle  \int_{[0,+\infty)^k} F(t_1,\dots,t_k)^2\ dt_1 \dots t_k \leq 1](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle++%5Cint_%7B%5B0%2C%2B%5Cinfty%29%5Ek%7D+F%28t_1%2C%5Cdots%2Ct_k%29%5E2%5C+dt_1+%5Cdots+t_k+%5Cleq+1&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)

and our objective (after a rescaling) is now to get

![Image 1115: \displaystyle  \int_{[0,+\infty)^{k-1}} \left(\int_0^\infty g(t_k) 1_{t_1+\dots+t_k \leq k}\ dt_k\right)^2 g(t_1)^2 dt_1 \dots g(t_{k-1})^2 dt_{k-1}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle++%5Cint_%7B%5B0%2C%2B%5Cinfty%29%5E%7Bk-1%7D%7D+%5Cleft%28%5Cint_0%5E%5Cinfty+g%28t_k%29+1_%7Bt_1%2B%5Cdots%2Bt_k+%5Cleq+k%7D%5C+dt_k%5Cright%29%5E2+g%28t_1%29%5E2+dt_1+%5Cdots+g%28t_%7Bk-1%7D%29%5E2+dt_%7Bk-1%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)

![Image 1116: \displaystyle  > 4m.](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle++%3E+4m.&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)

The emergence of the function ![Image 1117: {g}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bg%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) as a parameter in the multidimensional sieve is a feature that is not present in previous sieves (at least not without sharply curtailing the support of ![Image 1118: {g}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bg%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)), and is the main reason why this sieve performs better than previous sieves for this problem.

We interpret the above optimisation problem probabilistically. Let ![Image 1119: {X_1,X_2,\dots}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BX_1%2CX_2%2C%5Cdots%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) be independent real values in ![Image 1120: {[0,+\infty)}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5B0%2C%2B%5Cinfty%29%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) with probability density function ![Image 1121: {g(t)^2\ dt}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bg%28t%29%5E2%5C+dt%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002). Our task is now to obtain the inequality

![Image 1122: \displaystyle  \mathop{\bf E} \left(\int_0^{\min(k-X_1-\dots-X_k),0} g(t_k)\ dt_k\right)^2 > 4m.](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle++%5Cmathop%7B%5Cbf+E%7D+%5Cleft%28%5Cint_0%5E%7B%5Cmin%28k-X_1-%5Cdots-X_k%29%2C0%7D+g%28t_k%29%5C+dt_k%5Cright%29%5E2+%3E+4m.&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)

Suppose that we can locate a fixed function ![Image 1123: {g}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bg%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) (independent of ![Image 1124: {k}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bk%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)) obeying [(47)](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/#gat) with [](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/)

[![Image 1125: \displaystyle \mathop{\bf E} X_i = \int_0^\infty t g(t)^2\ dt < 1 \ \ \ \ \ (48)](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle++%5Cmathop%7B%5Cbf+E%7D+X_i+%3D+%5Cint_0%5E%5Cinfty+t+g%28t%29%5E2%5C+dt+%3C+1+%5C+%5C+%5C+%5C+%5C+%2848%29&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/)
[](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/) and [](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/)

[![Image 1126: \displaystyle \int_0^\infty g(t)\ dt = \infty \ \ \ \ \ (49)](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle++%5Cint_0%5E%5Cinfty+g%28t%29%5C+dt+%3D+%5Cinfty+%5C+%5C+%5C+%5C+%5C+%2849%29&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/)
[](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/) Then from the law of large numbers, ![Image 1127: {k-X_1-\dots-X_k}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bk-X_1-%5Cdots-X_k%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) goes to infinity almost surely as ![Image 1128: {k}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bk%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) to ![Image 1129: {\infty}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Cinfty%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002), and so from dominated convergence one has

![Image 1130: \displaystyle  \lim_{k \rightarrow \infty} \mathop{\bf E} (\int_0^{\min(k-X_1-\dots-X_k),0} g(t_k)\ dt_k)^2 = \infty](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle++%5Clim_%7Bk+%5Crightarrow+%5Cinfty%7D+%5Cmathop%7B%5Cbf+E%7D+%28%5Cint_0%5E%7B%5Cmin%28k-X_1-%5Cdots-X_k%29%2C0%7D+g%28t_k%29%5C+dt_k%29%5E2+%3D+%5Cinfty&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)

and the claim follows from [(49)](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/#dor-2) if ![Image 1131: {k}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bk%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) is sufficiently large depending on ![Image 1132: {m}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bm%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002).

We are thus reduced to the easy one-dimensional problem of producing a smooth function ![Image 1133: {g: [0,+\infty) \rightarrow {\bf R}}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bg%3A+%5B0%2C%2B%5Cinfty%29+%5Crightarrow+%7B%5Cbf+R%7D%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) obeying the constraints [(47)](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/#gat), [(48)](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/#dor2), [(49)](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/#dor-2). However, one can verify that the choice

![Image 1134: \displaystyle  g(t) := \frac{1}{(1+t) \log(2+t)}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle++g%28t%29+%3A%3D+%5Cfrac%7B1%7D%7B%281%2Bt%29+%5Clog%282%2Bt%29%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)

(barely) obeys [(49)](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/#dor-2) with ![Image 1135: {\int_0^\infty g(t)^2\ dt}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Cint_0%5E%5Cinfty+g%28t%29%5E2%5C+dt%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) and ![Image 1136: {\int_0^\infty t g(t)^2\ dt}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Cint_0%5E%5Cinfty+t+g%28t%29%5E2%5C+dt%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) both finite, and the claim follows by a routine rescaling.

> **Exercise 42** By working through a more quantitative version of the above argument, establish Theorem [38](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/#may) with ![Image 1137: {m \gg \log k}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bm+%5Cgg+%5Clog+k%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) as ![Image 1138: {k \rightarrow \infty}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bk+%5Crightarrow+%5Cinfty%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002). (_Hint:_ One can replace the use of the law of large numbers with Chebyshev’s inequality.) It is not currently known how to obtain this theorem with any choice of ![Image 1139: {m}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bm%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) that grows faster than logarithmically in ![Image 1140: {k}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bk%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002); the current record is ![Image 1141: {m = (\frac{1}{4} + \frac{7}{600}) \log k + O(1)}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bm+%3D+%28%5Cfrac%7B1%7D%7B4%7D+%2B+%5Cfrac%7B7%7D%7B600%7D%29+%5Clog+k+%2B+O%281%29%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002), [due to the Polymath project](http://www.resmathsci.com/content/1/1/12). It is known, though, that bounds on the order of ![Image 1142: {\log k}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Clog+k%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) are the limit of the Selberg sieve method, and one either needs new sieves, or new techniques beyond sieve theory, to increase ![Image 1143: {m}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bm%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) beyond this rate; see [the Polymath paper](http://www.resmathsci.com/content/1/1/12) for further discussion.

One can use the multidimensional sieve to study the classical sieving problem of counting prime ![Image 1144: {k}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bk%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)-tuples, but unfortunately the numerology it gives is precisely the same as the numerology given by previous sieves:

> **Exercise 43** Let ![Image 1145: {(h_1,\dots,h_k)}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%28h_1%2C%5Cdots%2Ch_k%29%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) be an admissible tuple, and let ![Image 1146: {w}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bw%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) go to infinity sufficiently slowly as ![Image 1147: {x \rightarrow \infty}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bx+%5Crightarrow+%5Cinfty%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002).

**— 4. The large sieve —**

In the general discussion of sieving problems such as Problem [3](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/#sivp) in previous sections, there was no structure imposed on the sets ![Image 1148: {E_p}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BE_p%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) beyond the fact that one could control the sums [(9)](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/#xd-sum). However, as we have seen in practice, ![Image 1149: {E_p}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BE_p%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) is typically the union of some number ![Image 1150: {\omega(p)}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Comega%28p%29%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) of residue classes modulo ![Image 1151: {p}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bp%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002). It is possible to exploit this structure using Fourier analysis, leading to an approach to sieving known as the _large sieve_, and relying crucially on the analytic large sieve inequality from [Notes 3](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/10/254a-notes-3-the-large-sieve-and-the-bombieri-vinogradov-theorem/). The large sieve tends to give results similar to that of the Selberg sieve; indeed, the two sieves are in some sense “dual” to each other and are powered by the same sort of ![Image 1152: {L^2}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BL%5E2%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)-based techniques (such as the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality). However, the large sieve methods can offer sharper control on error terms than the Selberg sieve if carried out carefully. As the name suggests, the large sieve is particularly useful in the regime when the number of residue classes modulo ![Image 1153: {p}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bp%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) being sieved out is large, for instance as large as a constant multiple of ![Image 1154: {p}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bp%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002). (When ![Image 1155: {\omega(p)}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Comega%28p%29%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) is _very large_ – close to ![Image 1156: {p}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bp%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002), then there is another sieve that gives even sharper results, namely the [larger sieve](http://www.ams.org/mathscinet-getitem?mr=291120) of Gallagher, which we do not discuss further here.)

To apply the analytic large sieve to sieving problems, we need to exploit an “uncertainty principle” [of Montgomery](http://www.ams.org/mathscinet-getitem?mr=224585) (discussed further in [this previous blog post](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2011/12/31/montgomerys-uncertainty-principle/)), that shows that functions which avoid certain residue classes in the physical domain are necessarily spread out in the frequency domain. More precisely, we have

> **Theorem 44 (Montgomery uncertainty principle)**[](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/) Let ![Image 1157: {f: {\bf Z} \rightarrow {\bf C}}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bf%3A+%7B%5Cbf+Z%7D+%5Crightarrow+%7B%5Cbf+C%7D%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) be a finitely supported function, and let ![Image 1158: {\hat f: {\bf R}/{\bf Z} \rightarrow {\bf C}}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Chat+f%3A+%7B%5Cbf+R%7D%2F%7B%5Cbf+Z%7D+%5Crightarrow+%7B%5Cbf+C%7D%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) be the Fourier transform ![Image 1159: {\hat f(\theta) := \sum_n f(n) e(n\theta)}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Chat+f%28%5Ctheta%29+%3A%3D+%5Csum_n+f%28n%29+e%28n%5Ctheta%29%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002), where ![Image 1160: {e(x) := e^{2\pi i x}}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Be%28x%29+%3A%3D+e%5E%7B2%5Cpi+i+x%7D%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002). Suppose that for each prime ![Image 1161: {p}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bp%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002), there are ![Image 1162: {\omega(p)}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Comega%28p%29%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) residue classes modulo ![Image 1163: {p}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bp%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) on which ![Image 1164: {f}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bf%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) vanishes identically for some ![Image 1165: {\omega(p) < p}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Comega%28p%29+%3C+p%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002). Then for any ![Image 1166: {\theta \in {\bf R}/{\bf Z}}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Ctheta+%5Cin+%7B%5Cbf+R%7D%2F%7B%5Cbf+Z%7D%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) and any natural number ![Image 1167: {q}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bq%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002), one has [](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/)
> 
> [![Image 1168: \displaystyle \sum_{a \in ({\bf Z}/q{\bf Z})^\times} |\hat f(\theta + \frac{a}{q})|^2 \geq h(q) |\hat f(\theta)|^2, \ \ \ \ \ (50)](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle++%5Csum_%7Ba+%5Cin+%28%7B%5Cbf+Z%7D%2Fq%7B%5Cbf+Z%7D%29%5E%5Ctimes%7D+%7C%5Chat+f%28%5Ctheta+%2B+%5Cfrac%7Ba%7D%7Bq%7D%29%7C%5E2+%5Cgeq+h%28q%29+%7C%5Chat+f%28%5Ctheta%29%7C%5E2%2C+%5C+%5C+%5C+%5C+%5C+%2850%29&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/)
> [](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/) where ![Image 1169: {h}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bh%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) is the multiplicative function [](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/)
> 
> [![Image 1170: \displaystyle h(q) := \mu^2(q) \prod_{p|q} \frac{\omega(p)}{p-\omega(p)}. \ \ \ \ \ (51)](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle++h%28q%29+%3A%3D+%5Cmu%5E2%28q%29+%5Cprod_%7Bp%7Cq%7D+%5Cfrac%7B%5Comega%28p%29%7D%7Bp-%5Comega%28p%29%7D.+%5C+%5C+%5C+%5C+%5C+%2851%29&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/)

_Proof:_ We may assume that ![Image 1171: {q}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bq%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) is squarefree, since otherwise we have ![Image 1172: {h(q)=0}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bh%28q%29%3D0%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) and the claim [(50)](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/#qqq) is trivial. Observe from the Chinese remainder theorem that if the inequality [(50)](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/#qqq) holds for two coprime values ![Image 1173: {q=q_1, q=q_2}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bq%3Dq_1%2C+q%3Dq_2%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) of ![Image 1174: {q}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bq%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002), then it also holds for ![Image 1175: {q=q_1q_2}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bq%3Dq_1q_2%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) (as every fraction ![Image 1176: {\frac{a}{q_1 q_2}}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Cfrac%7Ba%7D%7Bq_1+q_2%7D%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) in ![Image 1177: {{\bf R}/{\bf Z}}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%7B%5Cbf+R%7D%2F%7B%5Cbf+Z%7D%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) with ![Image 1178: {(a,q_1q_2)=1}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%28a%2Cq_1q_2%29%3D1%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) can be uniquely written as ![Image 1179: {\frac{a_1}{q_1} + \frac{a_2}{q_2}}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Cfrac%7Ba_1%7D%7Bq_1%7D+%2B+%5Cfrac%7Ba_2%7D%7Bq_2%7D%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) with ![Image 1180: {(a_1,q_1)=(a_2,q_2)=1}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%28a_1%2Cq_1%29%3D%28a_2%2Cq_2%29%3D1%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)). Thus it suffices to verify the claim when ![Image 1181: {q}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bq%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) is prime. By multiplying ![Image 1182: {f(n)}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bf%28n%29%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) by ![Image 1183: {e(-n\theta)}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Be%28-n%5Ctheta%29%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) we can also reduce to the case ![Image 1184: {\theta=0}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Ctheta%3D0%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002), thus our task is now to show that

![Image 1185: \displaystyle  \sum_{a \in ({\bf Z}/p{\bf Z}) \backslash \{0\}} |\hat f(\frac{a}{p})|^2 \geq \frac{\omega(p)}{p-\omega(p)} |\hat f(0)|^2](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle++%5Csum_%7Ba+%5Cin+%28%7B%5Cbf+Z%7D%2Fp%7B%5Cbf+Z%7D%29+%5Cbackslash+%5C%7B0%5C%7D%7D+%7C%5Chat+f%28%5Cfrac%7Ba%7D%7Bp%7D%29%7C%5E2+%5Cgeq+%5Cfrac%7B%5Comega%28p%29%7D%7Bp-%5Comega%28p%29%7D+%7C%5Chat+f%280%29%7C%5E2&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)

or equivalently

![Image 1186: \displaystyle  \sum_{a \in {\bf Z}/p{\bf Z}} |\hat f(\frac{a}{p})|^2 \geq \frac{p}{p-\omega(p)} |\hat f(0)|^2.](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle++%5Csum_%7Ba+%5Cin+%7B%5Cbf+Z%7D%2Fp%7B%5Cbf+Z%7D%7D+%7C%5Chat+f%28%5Cfrac%7Ba%7D%7Bp%7D%29%7C%5E2+%5Cgeq+%5Cfrac%7Bp%7D%7Bp-%5Comega%28p%29%7D+%7C%5Chat+f%280%29%7C%5E2.&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)

By the Plancherel identity in ![Image 1187: {{\bf Z}/p{\bf Z}}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%7B%5Cbf+Z%7D%2Fp%7B%5Cbf+Z%7D%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002), the left-hand side can be written as

![Image 1188: \displaystyle  p \sum_{b \in {\bf Z}/p{\bf Z}} |\sum_{n = b\ (p)} f(n)|^2 ](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle++p+%5Csum_%7Bb+%5Cin+%7B%5Cbf+Z%7D%2Fp%7B%5Cbf+Z%7D%7D+%7C%5Csum_%7Bn+%3D+b%5C+%28p%29%7D+f%28n%29%7C%5E2+&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)

and the right-hand side is

![Image 1189: \displaystyle  \frac{p}{p-\omega(p)} |\sum_{b \in {\bf Z}/p{\bf Z}} \sum_{n = b\ (p)} f(n)|^2.](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle++%5Cfrac%7Bp%7D%7Bp-%5Comega%28p%29%7D+%7C%5Csum_%7Bb+%5Cin+%7B%5Cbf+Z%7D%2Fp%7B%5Cbf+Z%7D%7D+%5Csum_%7Bn+%3D+b%5C+%28p%29%7D+f%28n%29%7C%5E2.&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)

Since the sum ![Image 1190: {\sum_{n = b\ (p)} f(n)}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Csum_%7Bn+%3D+b%5C+%28p%29%7D+f%28n%29%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) is only non-vanishing for at most ![Image 1191: {p-\omega(p)}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bp-%5Comega%28p%29%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) choices of ![Image 1192: {b}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bb%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002), the claim follows from the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality. ![Image 1193: \Box](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5CBox&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)

We then obtain the following variant of Theorem [32](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/#sieve-int) or Theorem [30](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/#ssu):

> **Theorem 45 (Arithmetic large sieve)**[](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/) Let ![Image 1194: {Q \geq 1}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BQ+%5Cgeq+1%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002). For each prime number ![Image 1195: {p \leq Q}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bp+%5Cleq+Q%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002), let ![Image 1196: {E_p}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BE_p%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) be the union of ![Image 1197: {\omega(p)}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Comega%28p%29%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) residue classes modulo ![Image 1198: {p}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bp%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002), where ![Image 1199: {\omega(p) < p}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Comega%28p%29+%3C+p%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) for all ![Image 1200: {p}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bp%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002). Then for any ![Image 1201: {M \in {\bf Z}}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BM+%5Cin+%7B%5Cbf+Z%7D%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) and natural number ![Image 1202: {N \geq 1}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BN+%5Cgeq+1%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002), we have [](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/)
> 
> [![Image 1203: \displaystyle |\{ n \in {\bf Z}: M < n \leq M+N\} \backslash \bigcup_{p \leq Q} E_p| \ll \frac{N+Q^2}{J} \ \ \ \ \ (52)](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle++%7C%5C%7B+n+%5Cin+%7B%5Cbf+Z%7D%3A+M+%3C+n+%5Cleq+M%2BN%5C%7D+%5Cbackslash+%5Cbigcup_%7Bp+%5Cleq+Q%7D+E_p%7C+%5Cll+%5Cfrac%7BN%2BQ%5E2%7D%7BJ%7D+%5C+%5C+%5C+%5C+%5C+%2852%29&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/)
> [](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/) where
> 
> 
> ![Image 1204: \displaystyle  J := \sum_{q \leq Q} h(q) ](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle++J+%3A%3D+%5Csum_%7Bq+%5Cleq+Q%7D+h%28q%29+&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)
> 
> 
> and ![Image 1205: {h}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bh%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) is given by [(51)](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/#hqor).

_Proof:_ Let ![Image 1206: {f}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bf%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) be the indicator function of the set in [(52)](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/#stabo). From Theorem [44](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/#mont-princ) with ![Image 1207: {\theta=0}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Ctheta%3D0%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002), and ![Image 1208: {q}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bq%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) summed up to ![Image 1209: {Q}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BQ%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002), one has

![Image 1210: \displaystyle  \sum_{q \leq Q} \sum_{a \in ({\bf Z}/q{\bf Z})^\times} |\hat f(\frac{a}{q})|^2 \geq J |\hat f(0)|^2.](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle++%5Csum_%7Bq+%5Cleq+Q%7D+%5Csum_%7Ba+%5Cin+%28%7B%5Cbf+Z%7D%2Fq%7B%5Cbf+Z%7D%29%5E%5Ctimes%7D+%7C%5Chat+f%28%5Cfrac%7Ba%7D%7Bq%7D%29%7C%5E2+%5Cgeq+J+%7C%5Chat+f%280%29%7C%5E2.&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)

Now observe from the basic identity

![Image 1211: \displaystyle  \frac{a_1}{q_1} - \frac{a_2}{q_2} = \frac{a_1 q_2 - a_2 q_1}{q_1 q_2}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle++%5Cfrac%7Ba_1%7D%7Bq_1%7D+-+%5Cfrac%7Ba_2%7D%7Bq_2%7D+%3D+%5Cfrac%7Ba_1+q_2+-+a_2+q_1%7D%7Bq_1+q_2%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)

that the [Farey sequence](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Farey_sequence)![Image 1212: {\{ \frac{a}{q}: q \leq Q; a \in ({\bf Z}/q{\bf Z})^\times\}}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5C%7B+%5Cfrac%7Ba%7D%7Bq%7D%3A+q+%5Cleq+Q%3B+a+%5Cin+%28%7B%5Cbf+Z%7D%2Fq%7B%5Cbf+Z%7D%29%5E%5Ctimes%5C%7D%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) is ![Image 1213: {\frac{1}{Q^2}}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Cfrac%7B1%7D%7BQ%5E2%7D%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)-separated in ![Image 1214: {{\bf R}/{\bf Z}}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%7B%5Cbf+R%7D%2F%7B%5Cbf+Z%7D%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002). We may thus invoke the analytic large sieve inequality (see Proposition 6 of [Notes 3](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/10/254a-notes-3-the-large-sieve-and-the-bombieri-vinogradov-theorem/)) to conclude that

![Image 1215: \displaystyle  \sum_{q \leq Q} \sum_{a \in ({\bf Z}/q{\bf Z})^\times} |\hat f(\frac{a}{q})|^2 \ll (N + Q^2) \sum_n |f(n)|^2.](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle++%5Csum_%7Bq+%5Cleq+Q%7D+%5Csum_%7Ba+%5Cin+%28%7B%5Cbf+Z%7D%2Fq%7B%5Cbf+Z%7D%29%5E%5Ctimes%7D+%7C%5Chat+f%28%5Cfrac%7Ba%7D%7Bq%7D%29%7C%5E2+%5Cll+%28N+%2B+Q%5E2%29+%5Csum_n+%7Cf%28n%29%7C%5E2.&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)

But from construction of ![Image 1216: {n}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bn%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002), the left-hand side of [(52)](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/#stabo) is equal to both ![Image 1217: {\sum_n |f(n)|^2}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Csum_n+%7Cf%28n%29%7C%5E2%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) and ![Image 1218: {\hat f(0)}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Chat+f%280%29%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002), and the claim follows. ![Image 1219: \Box](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5CBox&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)

Note that if one uses the sharp form of the large sieve inequality (Remark 7 from [Notes 3](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/10/254a-notes-3-the-large-sieve-and-the-bombieri-vinogradov-theorem/)) then one can sharpen [(52)](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/#stabo) to

![Image 1220: \displaystyle  |\{ n \in {\bf Z}: M < n \leq M+N\} \backslash \bigcup_{p \leq Q} E_p| \leq \frac{N+Q^2}{J}. ](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle++%7C%5C%7B+n+%5Cin+%7B%5Cbf+Z%7D%3A+M+%3C+n+%5Cleq+M%2BN%5C%7D+%5Cbackslash+%5Cbigcup_%7Bp+%5Cleq+Q%7D+E_p%7C+%5Cleq+%5Cfrac%7BN%2BQ%5E2%7D%7BJ%7D.+&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)

Setting ![Image 1221: {Q = N^{1/2-\varepsilon}}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BQ+%3D+N%5E%7B1%2F2-%5Cvarepsilon%7D%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002), one can then recover sharper versions of Theorem [32](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/#sieve-int) and its consequences, for instance by working along these lines (and using a very sharp version of the analytic large sieve inequality that exploits the variable spacing of the set of fractions ![Image 1222: {\frac{a}{q}}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Cfrac%7Ba%7D%7Bq%7D%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)), [result of Montgomery and Vaughan](http://www.ams.org/mathscinet-getitem?mr=374060) were able to completely delete the error term in the Brun-Titchmarsh inequality (Exercise [33](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/#bti)).

> **Remark 46** It is a general phenomenon that large sieve methods tend to give the same main term as the Selberg sieve; see [Section 9.5 of Friedlander-Iwaniec](http://www.ams.org/mathscinet-getitem?mr=2647984) for further discussion.

**— 5. The parity problem —**

We have seen that sieve theoretic techniques are reasonably good at obtaining upper bounds to various sieving problems, and can also provide lower bounds if the sifting limit is low. However, these methods have proven to be frustratingly ineffective at providing non-trivial lower bounds for the original problems that motivated the development of sieve theory in the first place, namely counting patterns of primes such as twin primes. While we do not fully understand the exact limitations of what sieve theory can and cannot do, we do have a convincing heuristic barrier, known as the [parity barrier](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parity_problem_%28sieve_theory%29) or _parity problem_ and first raised explicitly [by Selberg](http://www.ams.org/mathscinet-getitem?mr=53147), which explains why sieves have so much difficulty in lower bounding (or efficiently upper bounding) counts involving primes; in particular they explain why sieve-theoretic methods have thus far been unable to fully resolve any of the four Landau problems. The barrier is not completely rigorous, as it relies on the _Möbius pseudorandomness principle_ (or _Liouville pseudorandomness principle_) discussed in Section 2 of [Supplement 4](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/04/254a-supplement-4-probabilistic-models-and-heuristics-for-the-primes-optional/). As such, our discussion in this section will be somewhat informal in nature; for instance, we will use vague symbols such as ![Image 1223: {\approx}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Capprox%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) without defining their meaning precisely.

Consider the general sieving problem in Problem [3](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/#sivp), in which one tries to control the quantity ![Image 1224: {\sum_{n \not \in \bigcup_{p|P} E_p} a_n}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Csum_%7Bn+%5Cnot+%5Cin+%5Cbigcup_%7Bp%7CP%7D+E_p%7D+a_n%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) via knowledge of the sums ![Image 1225: {\sum_{n \in E_d} a_n}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Csum_%7Bn+%5Cin+E_d%7D+a_n%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002), together with the hypothesis that the ![Image 1226: {a_n}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Ba_n%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) are non-negative. In most situations, one expects this problem to be _underdetermined_; in particular, there will probably exist two different finitely supported sequences ![Image 1227: {(a_n)_{n \in {\bf Z}}}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%28a_n%29_%7Bn+%5Cin+%7B%5Cbf+Z%7D%7D%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002), ![Image 1228: {(b_n)_{n \in {\bf Z}}}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%28b_n%29_%7Bn+%5Cin+%7B%5Cbf+Z%7D%7D%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) of non-negative reals which are essentially indistinguishable with regards to the inputs of this sieve problem, in the sense that

![Image 1229: \displaystyle  \sum_{n \in E_d} a_n \approx \sum_{n \in E_d} b_n \approx X_d](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle++%5Csum_%7Bn+%5Cin+E_d%7D+a_n+%5Capprox+%5Csum_%7Bn+%5Cin+E_d%7D+b_n+%5Capprox+X_d&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)

for all ![Image 1230: {d \in {\mathcal D}}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bd+%5Cin+%7B%5Cmathcal+D%7D%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002), but which have very different outputs for this sieve problem, in that

![Image 1231: \displaystyle  \sum_{n \not \in \bigcup_{p|P} E_p} a_n \not \approx \sum_{n \not \in \bigcup_{p|P} E_p} b_n .](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle++%5Csum_%7Bn+%5Cnot+%5Cin+%5Cbigcup_%7Bp%7CP%7D+E_p%7D+a_n+%5Cnot+%5Capprox+%5Csum_%7Bn+%5Cnot+%5Cin+%5Cbigcup_%7Bp%7CP%7D+E_p%7D+b_n+.&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)

Then any upper bound for Problem [3](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/#sivp) with this choice of ![Image 1232: {E_d, X_d, {\mathcal D}, P}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BE_d%2C+X_d%2C+%7B%5Cmathcal+D%7D%2C+P%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) must exceed (at least approximately) the greater of ![Image 1233: {\sum_{n \not \in \bigcup_{p|P} E_p} a_n}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Csum_%7Bn+%5Cnot+%5Cin+%5Cbigcup_%7Bp%7CP%7D+E_p%7D+a_n%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) and ![Image 1234: {\sum_{n \not \in \bigcup_{p|P} E_p} b_n}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Csum_%7Bn+%5Cnot+%5Cin+%5Cbigcup_%7Bp%7CP%7D+E_p%7D+b_n%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002); similarly, any lower bound for this problem must be (approximately) less than the lesser of ![Image 1235: {\sum_{n \not \in \bigcup_{p|P} E_p} a_n}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Csum_%7Bn+%5Cnot+%5Cin+%5Cbigcup_%7Bp%7CP%7D+E_p%7D+a_n%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) and ![Image 1236: {\sum_{n \not \in \bigcup_{p|P} E_p} b_n}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Csum_%7Bn+%5Cnot+%5Cin+%5Cbigcup_%7Bp%7CP%7D+E_p%7D+b_n%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002).

As a special case of this observation, we have

> **Proposition 47 (Abstract parity barrier)**[](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/) (Informal) Let ![Image 1237: {E_d, X_d, {\mathcal D}, P}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BE_d%2C+X_d%2C+%7B%5Cmathcal+D%7D%2C+P%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) be as in Problem [3](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/#sivp). Let ![Image 1238: {(a_n)_{n \in {\bf Z}}}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%28a_n%29_%7Bn+%5Cin+%7B%5Cbf+Z%7D%7D%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) be a finitely supported sequence of non-negative reals such that
> 
> 
> ![Image 1239: \displaystyle  \sum_{n \in E_d} a_n \approx X_d](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle++%5Csum_%7Bn+%5Cin+E_d%7D+a_n+%5Capprox+X_d&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)
> 
> 
> for all ![Image 1240: {d \in {\mathcal D}}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bd+%5Cin+%7B%5Cmathcal+D%7D%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002). Suppose that we have an additional “parity function” ![Image 1241: {\omega: {\bf Z} \rightarrow \{-1,0,+1\}}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Comega%3A+%7B%5Cbf+Z%7D+%5Crightarrow+%5C%7B-1%2C0%2C%2B1%5C%7D%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) with the property that
> 
> 
> ![Image 1242: \displaystyle  \omega(n) = -1 ](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle++%5Comega%28n%29+%3D+-1+&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)
> 
> 
> for all ![Image 1243: {n \not \in \bigcup_{p|P} E_p}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bn+%5Cnot+%5Cin+%5Cbigcup_%7Bp%7CP%7D+E_p%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) in the support of ![Image 1244: {a_n}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Ba_n%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002), but such that
> 
> 
> ![Image 1245: \displaystyle  \sum_{d \in E_d} a_n \omega(n) \approx 0 ](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle++%5Csum_%7Bd+%5Cin+E_d%7D+a_n+%5Comega%28n%29+%5Capprox+0+&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)
> 
> 
> for all ![Image 1246: {d \in {\mathcal D}}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bd+%5Cin+%7B%5Cmathcal+D%7D%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002). Then any upper bound for Problem [3](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/#sivp) cannot be significantly smaller than ![Image 1247: {2 \sum_{n \not \in \bigcup_{p|P} E_p} a_n}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B2+%5Csum_%7Bn+%5Cnot+%5Cin+%5Cbigcup_%7Bp%7CP%7D+E_p%7D+a_n%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002), and any lower bound for Problem [3](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/#sivp) cannot be significantly larger than zero. In particular, one does not expect to be able to show using such sieve methods that the support of ![Image 1248: {a_n}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Ba_n%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) contains any appreciable number of elements outside of ![Image 1249: {\bigcup_{p|P} E_p}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Cbigcup_%7Bp%7CP%7D+E_p%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002).

_Proof:_ (Non-rigorous!) For the statement about the upper bound, apply the previous discussion to the sequence ![Image 1250: {b_n := a_n (1 - \omega(n))}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bb_n+%3A%3D+a_n+%281+-+%5Comega%28n%29%29%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002). For the statement about the lower bound, apply the previous discussion to the sequence ![Image 1251: {b(n) := a_n (1+\omega(n))}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bb%28n%29+%3A%3D+a_n+%281%2B%5Comega%28n%29%29%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002). ![Image 1252: \Box](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5CBox&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)

Informally, the proposition asserts that if the sifted set ![Image 1253: {{\bf Z} \backslash \bigcup_{p|P} E_p}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%7B%5Cbf+Z%7D+%5Cbackslash+%5Cbigcup_%7Bp%7CP%7D+E_p%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) is sensitive to the parity function ![Image 1254: {\omega}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Comega%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002), but original pre-sifted sequence ![Image 1255: {a_n}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Ba_n%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) and the sets ![Image 1256: {E_d}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BE_d%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) are not, then traditional sieve methods cannot give any non-trivial lower bounds, and any upper bounds must be off from the truth by a factor of at least two.

In practice, one can heuristically obey the hypotheses of this proposition if one takes ![Image 1257: {\omega(n)}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Comega%28n%29%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) to be some variant of the [Liouville function](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liouville_function)![Image 1258: {\lambda(n)}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Clambda%28n%29%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002), which counts the parity of the number of prime factors of ![Image 1259: {n}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bn%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002), in which case the parity problem asserts (roughly speaking) that the sifting problem must allow both numbers with an even number of prime factors and numbers with an odd number of prime factors to survive if one is to have any hope of good lower bounds. For instance:

> **Corollary 48 (Parity barrier for twin primes)** (Very informal) One cannot use traditional sieve methods to resolve the twin prime conjecture.

_Proof:_ (Very non-rigorous! Also assumes Liouville pseudorandomness) As mentioned in the introduction, there are a number of different ways to try to approach the twin prime conjecture by sieve methods. Consider for instance the approach in which one starts with the primes ![Image 1260: {A}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BA%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) in ![Image 1261: {[x/2+2,x]}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Bx%2F2%2B2%2Cx%5D%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) and removes the residue class ![Image 1262: {E_p = 2\ (p)}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BE_p+%3D+2%5C+%28p%29%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) for each ![Image 1263: {p \leq \sqrt{x}}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bp+%5Cleq+%5Csqrt%7Bx%7D%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002). The quantity ![Image 1264: {\lambda(n+2)}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Clambda%28n%2B2%29%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) will then be ![Image 1265: {-1}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B-1%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) on the sifted set ![Image 1266: {A \backslash \bigcup_{p|P} E_p}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7BA+%5Cbackslash+%5Cbigcup_%7Bp%7CP%7D+E_p%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) (since ![Image 1267: {n+2}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bn%2B2%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) will be prime on that set), while the Liouville pseudorandomness heuristic predicts that

![Image 1268: \displaystyle  \sum_{n \in E_d} 1_A(n) \lambda(n+2) = \sum_{x/2+2 \leq n \leq x: n = 2\ (d)} 1_{\mathcal P}(n) \lambda(n+2)](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5Cdisplaystyle++%5Csum_%7Bn+%5Cin+E_d%7D+1_A%28n%29+%5Clambda%28n%2B2%29+%3D+%5Csum_%7Bx%2F2%2B2+%5Cleq+n+%5Cleq+x%3A+n+%3D+2%5C+%28d%29%7D+1_%7B%5Cmathcal+P%7D%28n%29+%5Clambda%28n%2B2%29&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)

is negligible for all ![Image 1269: {d \leq x}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bd+%5Cleq+x%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) (say). The claim then follows from Proposition [47](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/#ab-b). ![Image 1270: \Box](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%5CBox&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)

> **Exercise 49** (Informal) Develop similar parity obstructions for the other sieve-theoretic approaches to the twin prime conjecture. In the case when one is sieving out the integers by two residue classes ![Image 1271: {0, -2\ (p)}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B0%2C+-2%5C+%28p%29%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) for each prime ![Image 1272: {p}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bp%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002), argue that any sieve-theoretic bound must in fact be off from the truth by a factor of at least _four_. (_Hint:_ experiment with weights such as ![Image 1273: {(1 - \lambda(n)) (1-\lambda(n+2))}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%281+-+%5Clambda%28n%29%29+%281-%5Clambda%28n%2B2%29%29%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002).)

> **Exercise 50** (Informal) Argue why any sieve-theoretic upper bound on prime ![Image 1274: {k}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bk%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)-tuples that is based on equidistribution estimates for the natural numbers and primes must be off from the truth by a factor of at least ![Image 1275: {2^{k-1}}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B2%5E%7Bk-1%7D%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002). (This should be compared with the known upper bounds of ![Image 1276: {2^k k!}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B2%5Ek+k%21%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) and ![Image 1277: {4^{k-1} (k-1)!}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B4%5E%7Bk-1%7D+%28k-1%29%21%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) discussed in previous sections. It is not clear at present which of these bounds is closest to the true limit of sieve theory.)

The above argument is far from watertight, and there have been successful attempts to circumvent the parity barrier. For instance:

*   (i) The parity barrier does not prohibit the possibility of producing a very small number of prime (or near-prime) patterns, even if one cannot hope to get lower bounds that are of the “right” order of magnitude. For instance, it was shown unconditionally [by Goldston, Graham, Pintz, and Yildirim](http://www.ams.org/mathscinet-getitem?mr=2806510) that there exist infinitely many pairs of consecutive numbers ![Image 1278: {n,n+1}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bn%2Cn%2B1%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002), each of which are the product of exactly four primes. Cramér type random models (see [Supplement 4](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/04/254a-supplement-4-probabilistic-models-and-heuristics-for-the-primes-optional/)) predict the number of such pairs up to ![Image 1279: {x}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bx%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) to be approximately ![Image 1280: {\frac{x}{\log^2 x}}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Cfrac%7Bx%7D%7B%5Clog%5E2+x%7D%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) (ignoring some factors of ![Image 1281: {\log\log x}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Clog%5Clog+x%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002)), and the parity barrier prevents one from getting lower bounds of this magnitude; but the construction of Goldston, Graham, Pintz, and Yildirim only produces a quite sparse set of pairs (about ![Image 1282: {\frac{x}{\log^3 x}}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Cfrac%7Bx%7D%7B%5Clog%5E3+x%7D%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) or so) and so evades the parity barrier. Using a very different class of arguments, it is also possible to produce analogues of patterns like twin primes in the function field setting (in which the role of primes are replaced by irreducible polynomials over a finite field) by using very sparse sequences of polynomials for which irreducibility can be tested easily; see for instance [this paper of Hall](http://www.ams.org/mathscinet-getitem?mr=2228953) for the case of twin irreducible polynomials. 
*   (ii) The parity barrier may disappear if one is somehow in a situation in which the Möbius or Liouville pseudorandomness principles break down. An extreme example of this is when one assumes the existence of one (or many) _Siegel zeroes_, which imply extremely high correlation between the Möbius or Liouville functions and a Dirichlet character, in strong violation of the pseudorandomness principle. A striking example of this case occurs in [this paper of Heath-Brown](http://www.ams.org/mathscinet-getitem?mr=703977), which demonstrates the truth of the twin prime conjecture if one assumes the existence of an infinite sequence of Siegel zeroes. 
*   (iii) Traditional sieve theoretic methods only assume that one can control “linear” or “Type I” sums of the form ![Image 1283: {\sum_{n \in E_d} a_n}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Csum_%7Bn+%5Cin+E_d%7D+a_n%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002). However, in some cases it is also possible to control “bilinear” or “Type II” sums such as ![Image 1284: {\sum_n \sum_m \alpha_n \beta_m a_{nm}}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Csum_n+%5Csum_m+%5Calpha_n+%5Cbeta_m+a_%7Bnm%7D%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) for various coefficients ![Image 1285: {\alpha_n, \beta_m}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Calpha_n%2C+%5Cbeta_m%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) of controlled size and support but uncontrolled phase. Note that multiplicative parity functions such as ![Image 1286: {\lambda}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Clambda%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) will be distinguishable with such sums, since ![Image 1287: {\sum_n \sum_m \alpha_n \beta_m \lambda(nm)}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Csum_n+%5Csum_m+%5Calpha_n+%5Cbeta_m+%5Clambda%28nm%29%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) will not exhibit any cancellation if ![Image 1288: {\alpha_n, \beta_m}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Calpha_n%2C+%5Cbeta_m%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) both oscillate with the sign of ![Image 1289: {\lambda(n), \lambda(m)}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Clambda%28n%29%2C+%5Clambda%28m%29%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) respectively; as such, the parity barrier is no longer in effect. In such situations it can be possible to go beyond the limits of traditional sieve theory, for instance by using decompositions similar to the Vaughan identity employed in [Notes 3](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/10/254a-notes-3-the-large-sieve-and-the-bombieri-vinogradov-theorem/). Of course, one still has to actually establish the required bilinear sum asymptotics, and this typically requires some deep and non-trivial input outside of sieve theory. A model example here is [the work of Friedlander and Iwaniec](http://www.ams.org/mathscinet-getitem?mr=1432648) establishing the infinitude of primes of the form ![Image 1290: {a^2+b^4}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Ba%5E2%2Bb%5E4%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002). Here, the relevant bilinear input is obtained using the algebraic structure of the Gaussian integers and some non-trivial results on lattice counting problems. 
*   (iv) Sieve theory is focused on “one-dimensional” sums, such as ![Image 1291: {\sum_{n \not \in \bigcup_{p|P} E_p} a_n}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Csum_%7Bn+%5Cnot+%5Cin+%5Cbigcup_%7Bp%7CP%7D+E_p%7D+a_n%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002), or perhaps ![Image 1292: {\sum_{n \leq x} \Lambda(n) \Lambda(n+2)}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Csum_%7Bn+%5Cleq+x%7D+%5CLambda%28n%29+%5CLambda%28n%2B2%29%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002). The presence of the parity problem relies on the ability to modify weights such as ![Image 1293: {a_n}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Ba_n%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) or ![Image 1294: {\Lambda(n)}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5CLambda%28n%29%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) in such sums in such a fashion that the value of the sum dramatically changes. If one instead is trying to control a _multi-dimensional_ sum, such as ![Image 1295: {\sum_{n,r \leq x} \Lambda(n) \Lambda(n+r) \Lambda(n+2r) \Lambda(n+3r)}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Csum_%7Bn%2Cr+%5Cleq+x%7D+%5CLambda%28n%29+%5CLambda%28n%2Br%29+%5CLambda%28n%2B2r%29+%5CLambda%28n%2B3r%29%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) (which roughly speaking counts arithmetic progressions of primes of length four), then there is no easy way to modify any of the factors of such a sum in the fashion required for the parity barrier to be in effect. For such multi-dimensional sums one can then hope to use additional estimates to produce non-trivial lower bounds. For instance, [with Ben Green, we used](http://www.ams.org/mathscinet-getitem?mr=2415379) Selberg sieves combined with the additional tool of [Szemerédi’s theorem](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Szemer%C3%A9di%27s_theorem) to obtain lower bounds for multi-dimensional sums similar to the one above, proving in particular that the primes contained arbitrarily long arithmetic progressions. 

It is one of the major problems in analytic prime number theory to continue to find more ways to defeat the parity barrier, so that we can produce prime patterns in many more situations than what is currently possible. However, this has proven to be a surprisingly challenging task, and one which typically requires the injection of new ideas and methods external to sieve theory.

> **Remark 51** As mentioned previously, it is not known in general if the parity problem represents the _only_ obstruction to sieve methods. However, under the additional hypothesis of the Elliott-Halberstam conjecture, and when focusing on “binary” problems such as the twin prime conjecture, there is a more detailed analysis of the parity problem [due to Bombieri](http://www.ams.org/mathscinet-getitem?mr=491570) which indicates that, in some sense, the parity problem _is_ indeed the only information not captured by sieve-theoretic methods, in that the asymptotic distribution of primes or almost primes in pairs ![Image 1296: {n,n+2}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bn%2Cn%2B2%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) is completely determined by a single parameter ![Image 1297: {0 \leq \delta \leq 2}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B0+%5Cleq+%5Cdelta+%5Cleq+2%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) that represents the ratio between the actual number of twin primes and the expected number of twin primes; the parity problem prevents us from obtaining any further information about ![Image 1298: {\delta}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Cdelta%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) other than that it lies between ![Image 1299: {0}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B0%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) and ![Image 1300: {2}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B2%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002), but once ![Image 1301: {\delta}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7B%5Cdelta%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) is known, essentially all other asymptotics regarding the prime factorisation of ![Image 1302: {n}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bn%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) and ![Image 1303: {n+2}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bn%2B2%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) will also be known. See Chapter 16 of [Friedlander-Iwaniec](http://www.ams.org/mathscinet-getitem?mr=2647984) for further discussion.

> **Remark 52** For a slightly different way to formalise the parity problem, see [this previous blog post](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2014/11/21/a-general-parity-problem-obstruction/). Among other things, it is shown in that post that the parity problem prevents one from proving Theorem [38](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2015/01/21/254a-notes-4-some-sieve-theory/#may) with any choice of ![Image 1304: {m,k}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bm%2Ck%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) with ![Image 1305: {m > \lceil \frac{k}{2} \rceil}](https://s0.wp.com/latex.php?latex=%7Bm+%3E+%5Clceil+%5Cfrac%7Bk%7D%7B2%7D+%5Crceil%7D&bg=ffffff&fg=000000&s=0&c=20201002) using purely sieve-theoretic methods, in particular ruling out a solution to the twin prime conjecture by sieve methods.
